PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES

INFORMATION NOTE
What this Part is about: This Part contains rules calculating time and
various administrative matters, including the filing of documents and
certification of copies of original documents. The Part also deals with the
contents of pleadings, affidavits, exhibits, payments into and out of Court,
and the responsibilities of Court officers and Court reporters.
Words and phrases used in this Part that have defined meanings in the
Appendix [Definitions] include: assessment officer; Attorney General for
Alberta; Chief Justice; claim; commencement document; Court; court
clerk; court reporter; defendant; enactment; file; judge; judgment; judicial
centre; lawyer; order; party; plaintiff; pleading; prescribed form; property;
record; remedy; rules; trustee
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES

13-3
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.1
DIVISION 1
JUDGE UNABLE TO CONTINUE
When One Judge May Act in Place of or Replace Another
13.1 One judge may act in place of or replace another judge if
(a) that other judge dies,
(b) that other judge ceases to be a judge, or
(c) it is inconvenient, improper, inappropriate or impossible for that
other judge to act.¹
Information Note
Section 7 of the Court of King’s Bench Act also provides for a judge to give judgment in a
case fully heard by him or her for up to 3 months after ceasing to hold office. Section 7 reads:
Judgment by former judge

Footnote
7 If a judge ceases to hold office without giving a judgment in any matter that was
fully heard by him or her, the judge may, within 3 months after ceasing to hold office,
give judgment in that matter as if the judge were still a judge of the Court and that
judgment has the same effect as though given by a judge of the Court.
Section 18(3) of the Interpretation Act may also be applicable; it reads:
(3) If an enactment provides that a proceeding, matter or thing is to be done by or before
a judge, the proceeding, matter or thing, if properly commenced before a judge, may
be continued or completed before any other judge of the same court.

Related Provisions
1.4(2) (i) (whether judge seized) ; 4.14 (authority of case management judge) ; 4.15 (restrictions

on case management judges); 7.10 (summary trial judge can remain seized).
A. Finishing Incomplete Work
Legislation gives a judge who retires, some months to sign judgments after he or she retires.²
But the hearing must be before retirement. Where one justice of appeal had retired (breaking the
quorum), the Chief Justice was asked to act under the predecessor Rule and to appoint a third
member of the Court of Appeal panel to let the panel reconsider the costs.³
Section 18(3) of the Alberta Interpretation Act says that anything properly commenced before
a judge may be continued or completed before any other judge of the same court.⁴
* The word “technical” is apt to mislead. Many parts of Chapter 13 are very important.

1883 O.72 r.3.

R.13.1 PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
Rule 13.1 can be used when the original judge is not ill, but has just developed a conﬂict of
interest.¹
It is doubtful that R.13.1(c) applies just because a litigant suggests (or applies to declare) that
one judge is biassed, so that does not give any other judge (additional) jurisdiction.²
This R.13.1 did not allow one judge to give judgment after a different judge heard some of the
evidence in a trial (unless by consent of all the parties).³
If one trial judge hears the evidence but
becomes unable to continue, and the parties agree that a new trial judge will read the transcripts
and exhibits and then decide the case, on appeal from the decision on the merits, the Court of
Appeal still owes deference to the second trial judge who decided on the record.⁴
Transferring a judge from the trial court to the appellate court is no problem for completing
judgments in Alberta, because judges of the Court of Appeal are ex officio King’s Bench judges
(and vice versa). But appointment to some other court (such as the Supreme Court of Canada)
probably stops a judge from acting further in the ﬁrst court, if only it has jurisdiction. A regulation
letting higher trial court judges hear cases in the lower trial court did not assist, especially given a
statute then saying that the judge transferred had six weeks to give judgment.⁵
This Rule may allow one judge to approve the form of an order granted earlier by another
judge?⁶
Where one judge gives a stay of proceedings, another judge should not stay the stay, because
of Rr.13.1, 9.15 (setting aside) ; he or she should refer the motion to the original judge.⁷
Once
one King’s Bench judge orders trial of certain issues (as to a disputed settlement), another judge
cannot order that other issues tried before that or at the same time.⁸
A justice or an Applications Judge almost always acts merely as part of the Court of King’s
Bench, and not as a separate tribunal.⁹
Therefore, R.13.1 may only make express what would be
implied anyway. R.13.1 is important in the occasional case where a statute or the common law
seems to call for a later act or decision by the same judge who made the earlier decision in the
matter. Where a trial judge gives a nonsuit but that is reversed on appeal, the trial just continues
before the same trial judge.¹⁰

13-5
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.1
B. Disqualiﬁcation and Bias
1. References
The general question of when a judge is or becomes disqualiﬁed to hear a particular matter
because of apparent bias, is discussed in the Civil Procedure Encyclopedia and other texts.¹
Generally, see Canadian Judicial Council, Ethical Principles for Judges (2021) ; Halsbury’s
Laws of Canada: Judges and Courts (LexisNexis 2022).
2. The Tests for Bias
(a) Tests Generally
The legal test for bias is still reasonable apprehension, e. g. real likelihood or probability from
what the judge said or did. Actual bias is not needed, and the judge could be disqualiﬁed even if
there was no real bias in fact.²
The tests must weigh the factors for disqualiﬁcation realistically, and not be mechanical or
absolute;³
a signiﬁcant risk of partiality is necessary; mere appearances do not govern all other
factors.⁴
The test for conﬂict of interest or bias of a judge is the conclusion of a reasonable right-minded
person, fully informed of the correct facts, who has thought the question through, and looks at it
realistically and practically, not unduly suspiciously.⁵
The topic for that conclusion is whether it is
more likely than not, that this judge, consciously or unconsciously would not decide fairly.⁶
What
a reasonable person would think, is a legal standard, not a question which can be answered by a
survey or petition of or by a large number of people.⁷
A trial judge is strongly presumed not to be biassed, partly because of his or her oath and
training, and partly because he or she gives reasons for deciding. So the onus is on the party
alleging bias to show it.⁸

R.13.1 PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
Too lax a test for bias will not do much more for fairness, but will harm many other interests.¹
It
takes strong evidence to ﬁnd bias.²
Unnecessary recusal at times is tempting and seems to be a
quick solution, but that burdens other judges and can cause delay.³
And it wrongly rewards judge
shopping.⁴
Generally speaking, some fairly close and signiﬁcant connection to one of the parties or
counsel by business interests or close family ties will sufﬁce to disqualify.⁵
That is not presumed,
and must be proved.⁶
A close relative of the judge employed by a law ﬁrm acting on one side,
is not enough to disqualify the judge, if the relative did not appear before the judge and had no
connection with this particular ﬁle; that employment does not disqualify all the lawyers in that ﬁrm.⁷
Several connections between people, no one connection enough to disqualify, cannot be
sequentially strung together to disqualify a judge indirectly.⁸

13-7
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.1
(b) Special Situations
A party cannot disqualify a judge by threatening to sue the judge, if he gives no evidence
to support such a suit.¹
Nor can a party disqualify the judge by ﬁling a complaint against the
judge.²
Nor by repeatedly appealing the judge’s decisions.³
Nor do vague allegations by unnamed
persons sufﬁce.⁴
It is customary for a new judge to observe a “cooling-off period” of a few years during which
the judge will not hear cases in which his or her old law ofﬁce is involved; there is no set length
for that period.⁵
But connections or involvements years before the judge’s appointment as a judge
do not create a reasonable appearance of bias.⁶
Especially if this judge had not been personally
involved, and only the former ﬁrm and other lawyers were.⁷
Or where the personal involvement
was many years before and the judge recalled nothing about it.⁸
(c) Case Management Judges
The tests for disqualiﬁcation of a case management judge are the same as the tests for other
judges.⁹
The judge also has a duty to keep the suit moving, where the parties are not doing that
effectively.
Court of King’s Bench judges have a duty (under the new litigation philosophy) to change
courtroom culture and curtail unnecessary delay and schedule cases, and see which applications
have a reasonable prospect of success. The judge doing so will not be the trial judge, and need
not mince words, and may be direct and emphatic about misconduct; that is not bias.¹⁰
An unfavorable ﬁnding or order is no evidence of bias, especially in case management or
difﬁcult and high-conﬂict litigation, where conduct of the suit needs evaluation in light of “realities on
the ground. ”¹¹
If a party disappointed with a ruling had the right to a fresh judge, case management
would accomplish nothing.¹²

R.13.1 PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
In a binding Judicial Dispute Resolution, the one judge acted both as an arbitrator and as a
judge, but that does not give any reasonable apprehension of bias. A judge is presumed to act
judicially and to disabuse her mind of any matters learned which the law says to ignore.¹
3. Other Judges on Panel
If one judge on an appeal court panel disqualiﬁes himself or herself and does not sit, there
is no obstacle to the other judges’ sitting.²
Bias of one justice does not automatically stain other
panel members.³
Each justice of appeal reads the materials and laws argument and makes that
justice of appeal’s own decision.⁴
And conduct or events during the hearing cannot disqualify
panel members who did not know of them.⁵
After argument, one member of a panel withdrew from taking part in the decision on the
grounds of disqualiﬁcation. In one case, the other 2 members of the court were not able to reach
a unanimous decision, and so reargument before a different panel was ordered, given sec.8 of
the Court of Appeal Act.⁶
Modern cases suggest that a judge cannot form part of a court sitting on appeal from his or
her own judgment.⁷
A town and town solicitor are separate from the Subdivision and Development Appeal Board,
and need different counsel. Ofﬁcers of the town cannot be admitted to the Board’s decision-
making process. A relation of a lawyer to one body is not a relation to the other body.⁸
Former R.505(6), calling for leave to appeal from the judge to be appealed, did not create
institutional bias. It is presumed that the judge will use known tests for giving or withholding leave,
and costs appeals have an established test.⁹
If one judge gives leave to appeal and gives reasons,

that does not bar her from sitting on the appeal, even in the Court of King’s Bench where one

4. Judge Holding Opinions
A judge’s impartiality is presumed, and must be disproved.²
Holding opinions or knowing facts will not usually disqualify a judge.³
Opinions on points of law
deﬁnitely do not disqualify.⁴
Opinions or hobbies or interests, and connection with groups supporting views, but which are
not advocacy or political groups, may not sufﬁce to disqualify a judge.⁵
Having expressed opinions on the topic in a previous decision between different
parties will not disqualify a judge.⁶
Nor will having heard or ruled on matters by one
of the present parties.⁷
Even if it was later held incorrect.⁸
Nor will having heard an
earlier motion in the same case.⁹
Many attempts have been made in recent times to

R.13.1 PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
disqualify a judge because he or she has heard a similar case,¹
or has decided some other case
against the same party, but almost all have failed. That does not ﬁt within any of the recognized
heads of bias.²
If a litigant is repeatedly before the courts, it is likely, even probable, that he or
she will come again before the same judge.³
A trial judge is not disqualiﬁed just because a few
years earlier as a lawyer he had prosecuted the same accused.⁴
Previous dealings with the same
parties in other litigation do not constitute bias by a court or tribunal or its members (judges).⁵
Indicia or attitude of a statutory tribunal is not automatically bias, and an expert tribunal are
expected to have and use knowledge and expertise, and to know common industry practices,
terms of art, and ebb and ﬂow of relevant social circumstances, so long as that is generalized,
not speciﬁc.⁶
5. Prejudging Case
(a) General Test
A judge can be found biassed because of remarks which he or she makes in court, but the
test for that is strict.⁷
A cumulation of acts, such as remarks, and denying evidence and argument,
[Footnote 9 continued from prior page]
judge or applications judge does not indicate bias on his or her part, nor call for later recusal by that judge or
applications judge: Carbone v. Whidden 2014 ABCA 300, Calg 1401 0068 AC (Sep 15). A case management
judge cannot hear the trial, even with consent, despite the Practice Direction: L. N. v. S. M.2007 ABCA 258,

Uned 743. Review of when harsh or critical words by a judge against counsel create a reasonable

13-11
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.1
can be enough to be bias.¹
But court-room comments must be seen in their context of the
circumstances and the whole proceeding.²
Since impartiality is presumed, the onus of proof is on
the party alleging bias.³
Appellants should not lightly allege bias by the trial judge.⁴
The test is a closed mind favouring one side; mere concerns or doubts are not enough.⁵
Comments after the judge has read or heard all the evidence, and during argument by counsel,
are easier to justify.⁶
Indeed they are proper, so long as the judge is still willing to consider contrary
arguments.⁷
And “prejudgment” at the opening of a reserved decision is perfectly proper.⁸
It is vital
to see the context in which the judge’s remarks were made.⁹
Bias and undue intervention in a trial by the judge are distinct, but they do overlap, and
interference during cross-examination is especially dangerous.¹⁰
The cumulative effect of comments by the trial judge during the trial, and of parts of his or her
ﬁnal reasons for decision, can create a reasonable apprehension of bias and upset the result,
looking at the nature of the proceedings, the context, and the whole record, and being attentive
[Footnote 7 continued from prior page]
apprehension of bias, with examples. The issue is not impoliteness, but a closed mind, or cutting off full
argument: Nazarewycz v. Dool 2009 ABCA 70, 448 AR 1; Emmons v. Workers’ Comp. Bd.2024 ABCA 78,
Calg 2301 0031 AC (Mar 6) (¶ 24). See also Gerrow v. Dewar 2011 ABCA 348, Edm 1103 0197 AC (Dec 5).
Forgetfulness or confusion does not betoken bias: R. v. Smith 2009 ABQB 618. Criticizing counsel’s lack of
preparation is worse when it alleges habitual lack of preparation, but not bad enough to upset the decision:
Scheidt v. Scheidt 2014 ABCA 24, 566 AR 303.

to the facts and issues of the individual case.¹
Such apprehension of bias can come from unfair
criticism of one witness when the other side’s witnesses are not criticized for the same things,²
especially where some of the criticisms were not made by counsel,³
or where the criticism was of
manner and form, not substance,⁴
and where another witness on the same side as the impugned
witness had no such ﬂaws, yet her evidence was rejected for unconvincing reasons.⁵
Having previously been on the board of a non-proﬁt association which retained a law ﬁrm
now involved in this suit, with no contact with that previous retainer or individual lawyer, is not a
conﬂict.⁶
That the judge was a member of a defendant law ﬁrm before he was appointed to the
Bench about 12 years before, is not enough for a conﬂict.⁷
A counsel who was a law clerk in the
court in question six years before, does not disqualify a judge who was on the court then.⁸
Merely
knowing or being friendly with one of the counsel does not disqualify either; if it did, the proportion
of justices disqualiﬁed and the number of applications to disqualify, would become unworkably
high, maybe impossibly.⁹
(b) Examples
After an arbitrator gave his award (except as to costs), one party’s disclosing to the arbitrator
an earlier offer to settle may have contravened the Arbitration Act, but it did not create reasonable
apprehension of bias.¹⁰
The trial judge’s allowing or encouraging one counsel’s cross-examination of a witness about
perjury, was close to the line, but not over, and did not show that the trial judge was biassed.¹¹
But a judge’s telling one lawyer’s boss to take the lawyer off the trial does create reasonable
apprehension of bias.¹²
Most modern cases do not ﬁnd such prejudgment or animus bias, but a
few do.¹³
When some procedural motion requires a cursory examination of whether the claim seems
meritorious, the case management judge has to rule on that, and has to put some recitals of the
conclusions reached into the formal order, and doing so is not evidence of bias.¹⁴

A trial judge was correct to cite (charge) the accused at once with contempt for refusing to
answer questions, and to select a summary procedure, but convicting and sentencing at once
could have prejudiced the jury who watched all that.¹
Other examples are found in the C. P. E.²
Later breach of a statement by a tribunal chair that it would not assign certain classes of
its members to hear certain litigation, cannot alone create operative bias of those nevertheless
appointed, especially with a fresh Chair and fresh tribunal.³
Could deciding without hearing any argument be bias?⁴
Not calling on one party to argue, and
then ﬁnding for him or her is proper, and done every day.
A party should not allege to the court that 23 different judges are disqualiﬁed from hearing his
or her case; that is forbidden judge shopping.⁵
Judge shopping is improper.⁶
A tribunal chair had twice consulted the law ﬁrm about to appear before him and his panel,
and the chair would not disclose any details, disregarded recent binding law submitted to him, and
questioned the motives of counsel objecting, so the Court of King’s Bench quashed his decision.⁷
6. Results of Bias
The right to trial before a judge who is not biassed is fundamental to our system. If the judge
is found biassed, then generally speaking the ﬁnal judgment and all orders made in the course
of the trial by him or her are unenforceable.⁸
But the tests for bias are much stiffer after the judge
has taken part in a judgment (especially as part of a multi-judge panel), and satisfying the tests
for recusal before hearing may well not sufﬁce.⁹
Where the trial judge showed appearance of bias to an important witness, and that side
therefore lost, on appeal, that undid the trial judge’s credibility ﬁndings and necessitated a new
trial.¹⁰
Even if judges would ordinarily be disqualiﬁed for interest or lack of independence, if all the
judges are so affected, the doctrine of necessity lets them act.¹¹
But if another court could hear
the case and its judges would not have that conﬂict, then the parties should apply to that court.¹²

Bias of a tribunal or court must be raised promptly, not later, and not collaterally.¹
Granting a
last-minute request to step down would in effect be to adjourn that appeal.²
A party knowing the
facts cannot take the chance of winning or losing and then after the decision, raise bias.³
If counsel
feels that former employment of the judge creates bias, counsel should object at the time.⁴
A tribunal properly asked to recuse itself for bias can nevertheless properly select a non-
biassed panel and start the hearing afresh. And bias of one member does not “taint” all the
members who were on the initial panel. Nor does the second panel have to know the precise
grounds for the initial recusal.⁵
7. Miscellaneous
Canadian appeal courts increasingly notice how common it is for self-represented litigants
who have lost, then to allege bias and seek recusal.⁶
Are some websites or gurus telling such
litigants to do that?
Assignment of individual justices of the Court of Appeal to motions or to panels hearing
particular appeals, is doubly random (much of it before anyone could know which individual cases
would be heard).⁷
Where legislation allows making Rules, and the Rules require that one member of a panel
represent one industry or interest, that cannot be bias or ground to upset the decision, that one
of the members did come from that group. A known interest or point of view is not usually enough
to constitute bias in law.⁸
Generally, questions of bias or recusal are decided by the judge in question,⁹
even if the
objection was made to a different judge,¹⁰
but he or she may ask another judge to rule upon the

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.1
matter.¹
The motion (or appeal) to have a judge recuse herself affects the other parties, and must
not be run ex parte.²
If the right tests are used, on appeal from a question of judge recusal, the standard of review
is deferential.³
Civil Practice Note No.1, para.34 said that the case management judge could not preside at
the trial except with consent. But how to distribute the proceeds of a personal injury suit at the
end of the suit is not a matter of trial, and the case management judge can deal with proceeds
received by a settlement.⁴
Judicial review or appeal can send a case back to the same judge or tribunal panel, and no
bias by the latter is then presumed.⁵
A Master who was asked to withdraw on the grounds that he had been unresponsive during
argument, replied that this litigant had a very unstructured style of arguing, so that intense
concentration was needed to watch for any legally relevant argument buried in the irrelevant
matters.⁶
The usual test for upsetting a judge’s decision for bias applies also to administrative tribunals,
but it must be adjusted for the relevant context. If the legislation (e. g. a labor tribunal) calls for
a 3-person panel, one of whom must “represent” one interest, and another interest, that is not
operative bias. Direct interest in the outcome would be forbidden bias, but not indirect, nor having
taken some position or advocated some policy.⁷
On a municipal tribunal and who assists it, see the Spruce Grove case.⁸
If one party improperly reveals an offer to settle, that does not let the other party move to
disqualify the tribunal for bias.⁹
A person should not argue before a tribunal if that person is a part-time member of the tribunal,
recently retired from it, or about to become a member of it.¹⁰
C. Judicial Independence
The whole question of independence of the judiciary is discussed.¹¹

R.13.1 PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
Provincial legislation retroactively changing the substantive and evidentiary rules for suits on
one narrow topic is constitutional.¹
Military judges are independent and impartial, and can hear criminal trials. Such judges can
be removed for cause (by the Cabinet) only on recommendation of a statutory Inquiry Committee.
The fact that they are also members of the armed forces and subject to the chain of command
and open to prosecution for service infractions or offences, does not remove their independence
or impartiality.²
A multi-member (administrative) tribunal can let members who did not hear argument comment
on general or policy aspects of a decision, to those who did hear argument, so long as the latter
are free to make their own decision, and are not lower in the hierarchy than those who comment.³
And a tribunal can have valid guidelines without creating bias or lack of independence.⁴
The Court of King’s Bench can give judicial review of an administrative decision of the Chief
Judge of the Alberta Court of Justice (alleged to be made on improper grounds) ; the Provincial
Judicial Council does not have exclusive jurisdiction.⁵
And solicitor-client costs may be awarded
to the judge who successfully challenges such an administrative decision on grounds of judicial
independence.⁶
It is constitutional for legislation to force a judge to preside over investigative hearings.⁷

13-17
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.2
DIVISION 2
CALCULATING TIME
Application of These Rules for Calculating Time
13.2 This Division describes how to calculate periods of time and applies to
(a) these rules, and
(b) judgments and orders.
Information Note
The convention used in these rules is that
- time periods of less than one month are expressed in days and in multiples of 5, 10
or 20 days
- longer time periods are expressed in months or years.

Related Provisions
1.8(b) (application of Interpretation Act to time)

Statutory rules for appeals must be strictly applied. There is no general power to relax times
or procedural requirements which are in statutes, not Rules of Court, especially where one party
expressly raises and relies on the breach of the statute.¹
If an Alberta Court of Justice judgment is ﬁled in the Court of King’s Bench to enforce, limitation
periods are those for King’s Bench judgments and date from ﬁling in the Court of King’s Bench
(subject to a Ministerial extension of deadlines during the Covid pandemic).²
The 2020 Ministerial Order giving 75 more days under various litigation time limits, because
of the Covid pandemic, has no qualiﬁcations and its two parts are alternative, and need not both
be applicable. Nor is that Order limited to actions before it lapsed (on June 1 2020). The Order
applies if the 75 days were running while the Order was in force, and it applies if the period had
started but not ﬁnished before the Order came into force (March 17 2020), or if the period started
while the order was in force (Mar 17 to June 1 2020).³
Counting Days
13.3 When counting to or from an event or activity in days, the date on which the
event or activity occurs is not counted.
Information Note
The basic rule can be illustrated as follows:
Counting forwards
In rule 3.31(3) (a) [Statement of defence] the defendant must file and serve a statement of
defence in Alberta within 20 days after service of the statement of claim.

Footnote
1 So an appeal to the Court of King’s Bench was dismissed for failure to serve in time: Zimmer-Worker Est. v.
App. Comm. of the Alta. Workers’ Comp. Bd.2015 CarswellAlta 3129, JCE 1403 00907 (7 Oct ’15) (oral
reasons).
2 Zavers v. Magma Surfaces 2021 ABQB 952, 39 Alta LR(7th) 420 (¶’s 4-11).
3 Straight Vac Serv. v. Sunshine Oilsands 2024 ABCA 8, JCC 2201 0184 AC (Jan 10).

13-18
R.13.3 PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
If the effective date of service of the statement of claim is January 4 (the event referred to in
rule 13.3 [Counting days]), the statement of defence must be filed and served on or before
January 24. The day of the event - the date service of the statement of claim is effected - is
not counted.
Counting backwards
Rule 3.9 [Service of originating application and evidence] requires certain documents to
be filed and served on parties 10 days or more before the date scheduled for hearing the
application.
If the scheduled date is May 15, the materials must be filed and served on or before May 5.
The day of the event - the scheduled hearing day - is not counted).
The rule for counting months is different; see rule 13.4 [Counting months and years].
Holidays, Sundays, etc.
Note that where a time period expires on a Sunday, a holiday or a day on which an office or
place of business where something must be registered, filed or done is not open for business
during its regular business hours, section 22(1) and (2) of the Interpretation Act apply, as
follows:
Computation of time
22(1) If in an enactment the time limited for the doing of a thing expires or falls on a holiday,
the thing may be done on the day next following that is not a holiday.
(2) If in an enactment the time limited for registration or filing of an instrument, or for the doing
of anything, expires or falls on a day on which the office or place in which the instrument
or thing is required to be registered, filed or done is not open during its regular hours of
business, the instrument or thing may be registered, filed or done on the day next following
on which the office or place is open.
The Interpretation Act¹
is similar to this Rule. Where there is a time limit for doing something,
usually one ignores fractions of a day.²
Therefore, one does not count the day of the event which
starts the time running. Day number one is the next day.³
If a statute says “at least”, or “not less
than”, then one does not count the last day either.⁴
Otherwise one does count that day.
If proceedings must be taken within X days after some act, they cannot be taken before that
act!⁵
If the deadline for appearing in court under a statutory limitation of time falls on a day when
the courthouse is closed, then the Alberta Interpretation Act says it is sufﬁcient to do it on the next
day that is not a holiday, and so the application can be made on that day.⁶
Because the Covid epidemic interfered with courthouse access, a Ministerial Order extended
time to commence proceedings after statutory limits, for the 75 days from March 17, 2020 to June
1, 2020, and barred counting those 75 days in calculating times to sue. That is mandatory and
applies to time limits for commencing proceedings set by the Rules of Court.⁷
The Court of Appeal’s adoption of electronic ﬁling does not relieve parties of their duties to
read and follow the relevant Rules of Court and Court of Appeal Practice Directions.⁸

1 RSA 2000 c. I-8 s.22(3), (4), (6), (7).
2 But one can look at fractions to see which of two events occurred ﬁrst.
3 The one exception is computing the life of a statement of claim before it must be served or renewed.
4 Is there any conﬂict between the Rules and the Interpretation Act on that point?
5 Grant v. Thompson (Yukon Elect. ) (1906) 37 SCR 495, 499 ff.

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.4(1)
Counting Months and Years
13.4(1) When counting to or from a date in months, time is calculated from the
date on which the event or activity occurs in the month to the same-numbered
day in a subsequent or previous month, as the case requires.
(2) If the count ends on the 29th, 30th or 31st and there is no same-numbered
date in the subsequent or previous month, the count ends on the last day of the
subsequent or previous month, as the case requires.
(3) When counting to or from an event or activity in years, time is calculated from
the date on which the activity or event occurs in a year to the same-numbered
date in a subsequent or previous year, as the case requires.
(4) If the count starts on February 29th and ends in a year that is not a leap year,
the count ends on February 28th of that year.
[Alta Reg.124/10;143/11]
Information Note
For example:
(i) a time period of 3 months counted forward from an event or activity on January 15
ends on April 15;
(ii) a time period of 3 months counted forward from an event or activity on January 31
ends on April 30;
(iii) a time period of 3 months counted backwards from an event or activity on April 30
ends on January 30;
(iv) a time period of one year counted from an event or activity on January 15 of a year
ends on January 15 of the next year.
Rules or statutes often give relief if the last day to do something expires when the court ofﬁce
is closed, or on a holiday. Such legislation merely gives one more business day at the end. If the
closure was for several days, those days are not all added onto the time period, let alone added
on twice.¹
Rule 4.33(2) speaks of “3 or more years”, and R.13.4(3) on calculating by ﬁnding the day with
the same number applies to calculating years. Rule 4.33(2) sets the deadline at 3 years, not more
than 3 years. But the 3 years must have passed and do not expire until the end of the day with
the same number as the last helpful step. The plaintiff has all the anniversary day (with the same
number) to take another meaningful step.²
Variation of Time Periods
13.5(1) Unless the Court otherwise orders or a rule otherwise provides, the
parties may agree to extend any time period speciﬁed in these rules.
(2) The Court may, unless a rule otherwise provides, stay, extend or shorten a
time period that is
(a) speciﬁed in these rules,
(b) speciﬁed in an order or judgment, or

Footnote
1 Goodzeck Est. v. Goodzeck 2019 NWTCA 4, Y #A1-AP-2019-000002 (¶ 4).
2 Covey v. Devon Can. Corp.2020 ABCA 445, Calg 1901 0233 AC (Dec 8) (¶’s 12-16).

13-20
R.13.5(3) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
(c) agreed on by the parties.
(3) The order to extend or shorten a time period may be made whether or not the
period has expired.
Information Note
Examples of where the rules prohibit time from being extended by the Court or the parties
include:
- the time for service of a statement of claim under rule 3.26(3) [Time for service of
statement of claim]
- the time for bringing most originating applications for judicial review under rule 3.15(2)
[Originating application for judicial review].

Related Provisions
6.3n (adjournment of applications) ; 8.6n (adjournment of trials) ; 14.2 (extending time in

appeals); 15.6 (resolution of difficulty or doubt) ; 15.8, 5.9, 15.10 (transitional rules) ; 4.7
(adjusting dates).
This commentary is about the law. For some practical advice about time limits in general and
how to avoid getting caught by them, see Côté, Systematic Advocacy, Chap.2, Part H (Juriliber
2017).
A. Limits on Court’s Power Over Time
1. The Legal Rule
On legislation extending time because of the Covid pandemic, see R.13.3 n.
A court or a judge cannot change a time ﬁxed by statute,¹
unless the statute expressly gives
that power,²
e. g. express power to extend the time to appeal in the Divorce Act, or the time limit
for judicial review under the Workers’ Compensation Act. Or maybe if the statute contains an
implied power to extend, e. g. where the statute incorporates some of the Rules of Court?³
R.
13.5 is conﬁned to extending or shortening times set by the Rules of Court or court orders, or
agreements of the parties.⁴
If a statute allows the court to extend the appeal time only on an
application made within that time, a more general provision for extending times does not apply.⁵

281 AR 396, leave den (SCC 2001) 283 NR 398; Leung v. Smith 2002 ABCA 233, leave den (SCC 2003)
346 AR 400; Ruskin Est. (Waterhouse) v. Chutskoff Est.2007 ABQB 108, 414 AR 164; Tymchak v. Edm.
S. D. A. B. (#2) 2012 ABCA 22, 519 AR 295 (one JA) ; Kehewin Cree N. v. Mulvey 2013 ABCA 294, 556 AR
282; but cf. Strilets v. Vicom Multimedia (#2) 2000 ABQB 616, 274 AR 6.
2 And then only to the extent and in the time and manner which the statute expressly permits: Bahcheli v. Alta.
Secur. Comm.2007 ABCA 166, [2007] 9 WWR 33 (appeal to Securities Commission).

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.5(3)
Nor can the court or a justice extend time in, or relieve against breach of, a Regulation.¹
Nor can the court or a judge change a time set by a Rule which expressly says that R.13.5
does not apply to it, such as R.3.26²
or R.3.15(2).³
Aside from that, the scope of R.13.5 is very wide, and the Rule is an important slip Rule. It is
not conﬁned to times within which to take a step, and may be exercised by implication.⁴
Even if R.
9.15 maybe cannot be used, deadlines for “independent” medical examinations set by an order
can later be extended.⁵
Rule 1.5 is an important adjunct to R.13.5, and in effect often will cure late acts. And because
of R.13.5, often a consent order is not necessary, and a written consent to extend time will sufﬁce.
It should be ﬁled with the Clerk.
2. Examples
On extending time to appeal from an Applications Judge, see R.6.14.
Rule 13.5 lets a judge extend the six-month time limit to assess or review a lawyer’s account,⁶
or a deadline set by a judge for making costs applications.⁷
The Court of King’s Bench’s decisions at one time conﬂicted on whether there is a power
to extend the time limit for certiorari under the Rules of Court, that being R.830 which sets a
6-month time limit.⁸
Now see R.3.15(2) n. F.
The Court of Appeal discusses when an interlocutory order can later be set aside or varied,
even if it is by consent.⁹
Consent orders differ, depending whether they are procedural or whether
they settle the lawsuit.
Rule 13.5 may not apply to R.14.88, which itself names no time period.¹⁰
B. General Tests for Extending Time
The general principle is that times should be extended¹¹
if enforcement of the original time limit

13-22
R.13.5(3) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
would work serious harm to the party applying, or to the public, if any harm done by the delay or
extension to the opposite party¹
can be, and is, remedied by costs.²
If the respondent or defendant
knows that the proceeding has been commenced and there is little or no prejudice, it is error in
principle to dismiss the proceeding without considering using a statutory power to extend time.³
Strength of merits may overcome weak explanation for delay, or vice versa; but weak merits
and weak explanation may be fatal.⁴
A time extension may be (and often is) denied if the late step
(such as a notice of appeal) would be bound to fail.⁵
Times or Rules are waived only to do justice;
without merits no such goal is achievable.⁶
The effect of a procedural time limit cannot be intelligently assessed without looking at the real
consequences.⁷
Justice includes the need for ﬁnality and certainty, and the consequent likely prejudice to
the respondent from delay or belated challenges, especially in certain types of administrative
procedure.⁸
It is even harder to get time extended, if there is little explanation for the delay and no
demonstration of timely and continuing intent to apply.⁹
Time to appeal (or seek leave) may not
be extended for a party whom the courts below have found to be merely delaying and obfuscating
throughout.¹⁰
See further R.14.8n. B.
Rule 13.5 is more concerned with a reasonable explanation for the delay, than with knowledge
or its lack (thus distinguishing the Rules from the Limitations Act). Failure to realize the implications
of a fact may sufﬁce under R.13.5.¹¹

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.5(3)
This Rule can be used after the time has expired, even if the effect is to revive a dead action.¹
If a time period for an act is missed, the better remedy is to extend the time to do it, not to give
leave to commence a fresh proceeding.²
On the need for sworn evidence, see Part C below.
One case deals with this topic as a question of retroactive orders, rather than time extensions.³
Another case dealt with time limits as a question of dispensing with deadlines in court Practice
Notes, rather than extending times.⁴
C. Examples⁵
Very short delays are often excused, especially with a good explanation such as a very recent
unexpected big change in the procedures to be followed.⁶
Conversely, a time extension should be refused where it would have to be long, would likely
work irreparable harm to the respondent, and any harm to the applicant (from enforcing the time
limit) would be reparable later with money.⁷
Ordinarily time can be extended when bad legal advice caused missing a deadline;⁸
or where
there is no prejudice and some delay by the other party;⁹
or when the 6-month limit to restore an
unperfected appeal has expired;¹⁰
or where a late costs direction after a substantive decision is
sought.¹¹
But a court may well not extend time where that party’s case is weak and the proceeding
would be uneconomical;¹²
where the excuse is an ill lawyer and being busy with other things;¹³
or
where no ignorance of facts or inability to act in time is shown;¹⁴
and shown by sworn evidence;¹⁵

Footnote
1 C. P. E. , Chapter 18, Part P. Legislation on extension of time for an appeal, with express no equivalent of
R.548(2), should be interpreted as allowing an extension after expiry: Manyﬁngers v. Chief of Police 2006
ABCA 162. On the deadline (Friday) for answering the answers were given to someone to deliver, but by
misunderstanding were not delivered until Monday morning. There was no prejudice. The extension was
granted: Simmons v. Wickstron 2000 ABCA 77. And an appeal deemed abandoned can be restored: U.
Tractor v. Horseshoe Contr. (#2) 2002 ABCA 161, 303 AR 205. An appeal which is late and needs a time
extension should not be denied a 2-day adjournment to get an afﬁdavit to show grounds to extend the time:
Barker v. Sowa (#2) 2004 ABCA 93. See further the C. P. E. , Chapter 74, Part L.8.

2 SCR 294, 212 NR 230.

R.13.5(3) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
where the applicant has had years to get ready and had already had time extended once;¹
or in a
subdivision and appeal board appeal, which is presumed urgent.²
A request to ﬁle evidence over six months late was denied as the applicant had been moving
unnecessarily slowly and the hearing was only a week away and rebutting the new evidence
would take more months, and the events in question were over 40 years old.³
Since Crown corporations are not bound by limitation periods, an order for leave to take the
next step in a foreclosure suit was afﬁrmed despite 2 ½ years’ delay.⁴
Only matters clearly procedural depend on the lex fori, not limitation periods.⁵
Where the question is time to assess or review a lawyer’s account, different criteria apply to
the lawyer and the client, because of the differing nature of the two sides’ claims.⁶
Rarely should the Court of Appeal give permission to appeal an order setting or changing time
limits.⁷
A chambers decision on extending time under R.6.14 is discretionary, and is reviewed by
the Court of Appeal on the usual tests (reasonableness).⁸
D. Agreements and Waivers
Rule 13.5(1) does not apply in the Court of Appeal: see R.14.2(3).
So-called “standstill agreements” between counsel are not to be presumed to make the clock
stop, i. e. to add the length of the dead interval to the end of the original time period. Lawyers
should be more speciﬁc.
Promissory estoppel is more or less applicable, and it is revocable on reasonable notice. Such
estoppel becomes irrevocable only if the promisee cannot resume his position.⁹
What such an
agreement means is a question of fact in each case.¹⁰
The court need not extend times, nor ﬁnd
unfairness, where a party’s time waiver was terminated in ample time to allow the step to be taken
before the time limit in the Rules expired.¹¹
To establish promissory estoppel and thereby defeat a time limit, one needs a promise or
assurance intended to affect the legal relationship and to be acted on, plus the promisee acting
on it to change his or her position. Or a course of negotiation leading the other to suppose that
strict rights under the contract would not be enforced and that legal relations would be altered
accordingly.¹²
Mere silence will not create any estoppel or extend times where there is no duty to speak.¹³

Estoppel is not available against a statutory deadline where the trustee in bankruptcy has
a statutory duty to perform (distributing assets) for the beneﬁt of creditors. Nor does a court of

There cannot be waiver without full knowledge of the right alleged to be waived. If a defendant’s
insurer believes that the plaintiff has issued a Statement of Claim, then the insurer does not know
that it has a limitations right, and cannot waive it.²
On a previous contract waiving a deadline, see the Jarjoura case.³
On an earlier occasion, a consent order gave a defendant one last chance to turn up for
examination for discovery, with an automatic striking out of its defence if it did not. The defendant
did not come then, and a Master struck out its defence. But on appeal, a justice gave the defendant
another chance and reinstated its statement of defence, and the Court of Appeal afﬁrmed that.⁴
DIVISION 3
PLEADINGS
Pleadings: General Requirements
13.6(1) A pleading must be
(a) succinct, and
(b) divided into consecutively numbered paragraphs, with dates and
numbers expressed in numerals, unless words or a combination of
words and numerals make the meaning clearer.⁵
Information Note
Very large numbers are better expressed in a combination of numerals and words, for
example, $25 billion. The first word of a sentence is usually better expressed as a word
than a numeral. For dates, unless the pleading is clear about the convention being used, the
month should be stated as a word.
(2) A pleading must state any of the following matters that are relevant:
(a) the facts on which a party relies, but not the evidence by which the
facts are to be proved;⁶
(b) a matter that defeats, or raises a defence to, a claim of another party;
(c) the remedy claimed, including
(i) the type of damages claimed,

1 Tamglass Amer. v. Richter Allen & Taylor 2005 ABCA 341, 380 AR 286.
2 Johnston v. Carlos 2002 ABCA 127, 317 AR 155. Cf. Mema v. C. P. Accts. of Alta.2022 ABCA 4, Edm
2103 0138 AC (Jan 11) (¶’s 44-47).
3 B. M. v. Jarjoura, supra.
4 Custom Metal Installations v. Winspia Windows 2020 ABCA 333, 13 Alta LR(7th) 65.

R.13.6(3) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
(ii) to the extent known, the amount of general and special
damages claimed, or if either or both are not known, an
estimate of the amount or the total amount that will be
claimed,
(iii) a statement of any interest claimed, including the basis for
the interest, and the method of calculating the interest, and
(iv) costs, including any known special costs.
(3) A pleading must also include a statement of any matter on which a party
intends to rely that may take another party by surprise, including, without
limitation, any of the following matters:
(a) breach of trust;
(b) duress;
(c) estoppel;
(d) fraud;
(e) illegality or invalidity of a contract, including the grounds;
(f) malice or ill will;
(g) misrepresentation;
(h) payment;
(i) performance;
(j) release;
(k) undue inﬂuence;
(l) voluntary assumption of risk;
(m) waiver;
(n) lack of capacity or authority;
(o) wilful default;
(p) tender of payment;
(q) a limitation period;
(r) a provision of an enactment.
(4) If the defendant proposes to produce evidence about the plaintiff’s reputation
in mitigation of damages, the defendant must include in a pleading the facts on
which the defendant intends to rely.

Related Provisions
13.9 (defence of tender) ; 13.13 (requirements for filed documents).

A. Role of Pleadings
1. Introduction
Pleadings have many aims and effects. A good lawyer knows them, and can make his or
her pleadings work effectively. The rest of this commentary is largely on the law, but you can get
practical guidance on how to make pleadings work for you, in Côté, Systematic Advocacy, Chap.
5, including the checklist on pp.5-19 to 5-20 (Juriliber 2017).

13-27
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.6(4)
Generally, see Bullen & Leake & Jacob’s Precedents of Pleadings (19th ed. Sweet & Maxwell
2020); Pleadings: Principles and Practice (Sweet & Maxwell 1990) ; Rose, Pleadings Without Tears
(9th ed. O. U. P.2017) ; Bullen & Leake & Jacob’s Canadian Precedents of Pleadings (Carswell
2017).
2. Importance
Rule 13.6(1), (2) is one of the two pillars of our whole system of pleadings, the foundation for
all civil litigation. It appeared ﬁrst as England’s Judicature Act 1875, and then was later copied
in the Northwest Territories and Alberta. It abolished common-law pleadings, which mandated a
stereotyped set of assertions for a speciﬁc cause of action. Instead, a modern pleading is a simple
brief statement of the relevant facts.¹
Though one may name in a pleading a cause of action or a speciﬁc defence, usually one need
not do so.²
Rules 13.7 and following give some exceptions (necessary contents) for pleadings.
It is misleading and unfortunate that this Rule is in a Part called “technical”, for many reasons
given in this Part A. Pleadings are of key importance.³
Civil litigation does not turn the court into
a self-governed roving “commission of inquiry”, even in aboriginal matters. The court ﬁrst has
to know what the contentions of the parties are.⁴
Ordinarily a party cannot get relief or go into
issues, which are not in his originating pleading, and so an Originating Application seeking only
declaratory relief (and costs) will not permit them.⁵
A judge is not a roaming knight-errant in pursuit
of his or her own ideal of beauty or goodness.⁶
3. Effects of Pleadings
What is or is not relevant (and material) depends on all the pleadings in the suit; the facts
alleged to be relevant must have been pleaded by some party.⁷
Courts should generally avoid granting remedies not expressly or implicitly sought by the
pleadings, especially in commercial litigation. That is fair, as parties can amend their pleadings,
usually at any stage. The Judicature Act s.8 and R.1.3(2) create exceptions to that need for an

R.13.6(4) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
express pleading, but it takes a compelling reason to do that, e. g. the parties’ conduct all through
the lawsuit. Rule 13.6(2) (c) (iii) which requires that a claim for interest be pleaded, speciﬁes the
way that litigants are to invoke the jurisdiction of the Court.¹
An unpleaded remedy can be given
by amending the pleading, but only if there is no prejudice to the other party. Having to answer a
new pleading is not prejudice, but inability to do so may be prejudice, e. g. because of decisions
made in the meantime or positions changed.²
Pleadings shape the issues, and one cannot raise on appeal an issue not pleaded and
not in the evidence.³
Nor can one do so at trial without an amendment.⁴
Nor on an application
for summary judgment.⁵
Neither party can redeﬁne the issues without getting (or making) an
amendment to the pleadings.⁶
A judgment ordinarily cannot proceed on an issue not pleaded.⁷
That is error of law.⁸
That principle enshrines the ﬁrst rule of natural justice, the right to advance
notice of the case against one.⁹
Deciding on a ground not formally notiﬁed in writing becomes
worse if the tribunal itself raises the point during cross-examination, and does not give any extra
time to think about or rebut the new point.¹⁰
Pleadings also show whether the court has jurisdiciton.¹¹
However, sometimes a fairly vague plea, coupled with later notice (e. g. opening statement at
trial, or closing argument), and lack of objection, or admission of no surprise, bar objecting to the
fact that the issue was not very clear or speciﬁc in the pleadings.¹²
Breach of a physician’s duty to provide informed consent is a separate cause of action, not
part of medical negligence, and it has its own elements needing to be pleaded. An allegation that
the plaintiff would not have consented to the operation had he known the risks, was given only in
argument, not in pleadings, though it is a key element of this cause of action.¹³

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.6(4)
B. Prayer for Relief
It is customary to put at the end of a statement of claim or counterclaim a speciﬁc prayer for
relief, but R.13.6 seems to be the only Rule expressly calling for it. Doubtless a prayer is a good
idea, to prevent surprise and allow the correct column of Schedule C to be selected for costs.¹
Rule 1.3(2), which says that the Court can give a remedy “whether or not it is claimed or
sought in an action”, conﬁrms the wide remedial jurisdiction of the Court of King’s Bench, but
merely says that the Rules do not purport to limit the jurisdiction of the Court.
Most prayers violate R.13.6, but that is presumably harmless, especially as some courts have
interpreted the Rule narrowly or even overlooked it at times.²
The court can give any remedy
which it thinks ﬁt, even if that particular remedy has not been pleaded, given R.1.3(2) ?³
The word
“jurisdiction” is used in several senses, and limiting relief to what is pleaded is not truly a question
of jurisdiction, especially as the court can amend the pleadings, and slip Rules apply. Relief can
be given on conditions, even if the conditions are not spelled out in pleadings.⁴
If one wishes solicitor-client costs from the other side, it is a good idea to ask for them
speciﬁcally.⁵
When assessing damages against a defendant noted in default, the court assessed larger
damages than claimed, by amending the prayer for relief.⁶
If there is no danger to trial fairness, an
amendment to the prayer by the trial judgment before entry of the formal judgment may cure any
under-estimate. Sometimes even an implied amendment.⁷
Aside from general and special damages, other types, such as punitive damages, need not be
stated or estimated, given the wording of R.13.6(c), especially if the defendant did not object to
the omission of a speciﬁc number?⁸
A prayer for relief has no independent force, and is of no use to the plaintiff unless he or she
pleads and proves facts which will support it.⁹
If the prayer plainly has no foundation in the body

R.13.6(4) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
of the Statement of Claim, e. g. because all the foundation has been refused, then the prayer (too)
should be struck out.¹
The prayer’s contents or lack of them are relevant but not conclusive as to
the nature of the suit.²
Where the court’s jurisdiction depends on the amount in issue, the prayer for relief in the
statement of claim is not enough to establish that.³
Breach of R.13.6(2) (c) was among the reasons for not giving double costs under R.4.29.⁴
An injunction against the Crown and continuous court supervision are possible remedies that
cannot be struck out.⁵
C. Concision and Format
Avoid verbosity in pleadings.⁶
The previous Rules said “succinct” and the new say “concise”,
but it means the same.⁷
Repetition should be avoided.⁸
Prolixity is not often a ground in itself to strike out appealing, but combined with confusion and
inconsistency, it may be.⁹
If a statement of claim is not succinct, and contains evidence and argument, the court may
(instead of reviewing individual passages) say so, and then direct that the plaintiffs submit an
abbreviated version of the pleading.¹⁰
Separate allegations in a pleading do not need separate sentences or paragraphs. One
sentence often includes a number of allegations, or a list of various people alleged to have done
something.¹¹
They should be read distributively.¹²
No one style of pleading is mandatory, and though
it is desirable to recite related facts contiguously, that is not legally necessary, nor is failure to do
so ground to strike out.¹³
A pleading should not ordinarily have appendices or attachments (unless it is a defamation
statement of claim attaching the defendant’s lengthy words which are complained of); but the
Clerk should not refuse to issue or ﬁle such a pleading.¹⁴
It is proper for a statement of claim to give
quotations from the contract sued on, so long as they are not excessive.¹⁵
See also R.13.12 and notes to it.

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.6(4)
D. Defences or Rebuttals
Whatever its title, a document ﬁled with the Clerk is not a statement of defence if it merely
says that the defendant will defend (or will counterclaim) (and how to reach him). At best it is a
demand for notice.¹
Nor should a statement of claim say what the plaintiff would reply if the defendant were to aver
such and such in the later statement of defence.²
A statement of claim must set out the basis of
the claim, but it is not required to negate every possible defence or answer that can be made.³
If
it is needed, it can go in the Reply later ﬁled.
A statement of defence must not contain evasive responses on points of substance.⁴
Though usually only facts need be pleaded, R.13.6(2) requires pleading what defeats or
defends another party’s claim, and so resulting trust and relief from forfeiture should be pleaded,
as it could otherwise take the opponent by surprise, under R.13.6(3). Nor is resulting trust or relief
from forfeiture so common and routine as to be expected in any contractual dispute.⁵
Malice, ill will or the like must be speciﬁcally pleaded. A statement of claim alleging permitting
water drainage or ﬂooding seemed to allege negligence, and at trial the defendant was unfairly
surprised by arguments about bad faith. The action was dismissed.⁶
Limitations statutes have real and beneﬁcial purposes, and are not mere technicalities, so a
plaintiff who fails because of such a statute pays full costs ordinarily.⁷
Whether a limitation period
has expired may depend upon whether the relief claimed in the suit is essentially declaratory or
remedial (and then if some is remedial, whether it is severable).⁸
Also see Part H below.
If res judicata or issue estoppel is to be relied upon, it must be pleaded.⁹
E. Plead Facts Only?
A pleading need only plead the important facts which in law would create a certain cause of action
(or defence).¹⁰
It need not name the cause of action, nor name any cause of action, nor give a legal
conclusion, and indeed it is not fatal if it names a different cause of action, or the wrong cause of

action.¹
It need not negate every defence.²
Though it is not enough for a pleading merely to plead a legal conclusion without necessary
facts,³
a pleading should not be struck out for mere want of particulars.⁴
The remedy is to order
more particulars. Factual conclusions, such as the fact that one person has inﬂuence over another,
are facts which can be pleaded and should not be struck out.⁵
But it is vital to plead the facts triggering the desired point of law, e. g. , that the defendant’s
vehicle was facing a trafﬁc control signal displaying a red light alone.
The Rule states that a party must give the essential facts. More conclusions or legal propositions
do not sufﬁce. Statements of claim which merely stated conclusions, and gave no supporting facts
except who they were against, were severely criticized. They told nothing about what had been
done or when. A defendant cannot defend himself or herself against such a statement of claim.⁶
These statement of claims were held to be a prima facie indication of vexatious litigation.⁷
A
pleading baldly alleging fraud without facts or details, may well be ineffective and not raise fraud
as an issue.⁸
Some torts are complex, with a number of necessary elements (some alternative) pleading
facts to satisfy those necessary elements is important.⁹
Evidence, such as how one will prove something, is not to be pleaded.¹⁰

facts and evidence is a question of degree at times.¹
Nor should one plead argument.²
In pleadings, what is impermissible argument is a ﬁne line,
and not every bit of argument should be struck from a statement of claim.³
A pleading should recite facts directly, and omit phrases such as “the defendant says. ” Pleading
a point of law is optional (unless omitting it would surprise the other side, or a Rule requires it).
On pleading statutes, see Part H below.
Bad faith and dishonesty cannot be found and lead to judgment, if they were not pleaded
under R.13.6(3).⁴
The speciﬁc important requirements for a plea of defamation are listed,⁵
and they include the
full and precise words complained of.⁶
F. Authorities
Before drafting a statement of claim of any but the most routine sort, one should look at a
textbook on the substantive law (tort, contract, etc. ) to see what are the elements of the cause of
action. It is always advisable to refer to good published precedents of such pleadings. If English
and Canadian law are not too different, Bullen and Leake’s Precedents of Pleadings is probably
the best,⁷
and less bulky than Atkin’s Court Forms. It would be a brave court which would say that
a precedent from those books did not disclose a reasonable cause of action, unless the highest
Canadian courts have since clearly changed the law.
Conversely, copying a pleading from another local action is dubious. You do not know who the
ultimate author is, and it probably has no blessing from anyone.
G. Interpreting Pleadings
Maybe ambiguities in a pleading (other than admissions) are to be resolved against the pleader
at trial, but that is not a rule for striking out pleadings before trial.⁸
Indeed, pleadings should be
interpreted liberally and generously, even in a summary dismissal application.⁹
In a matrimonial case, the sloppy practice of one party in not responding to “concise letters”
setting out the issues, so the opponent incurred extra expenses, was a signiﬁcant error.¹⁰

R.13.6(4) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
If a plaintiff unnecessarily pleads gross negligence and proves ordinary negligence, and in law
that is enough, he wins. Pleading the greater includes the less and sufﬁces. It is not an admission
that the plaintiff has to prove the greater (i. e. , gross negligence. )¹
Statements by a party (plaintiff) in his pleading (statement of claim) are not evidence,
especially outside that suit, unless it is an admission against interest.²
One cannot treat a plea by
the defendant as one by the plaintiff.³
And a third party notice’s allegations are not admissions by
the defendant to the plaintiff.⁴
H. Afﬁrmative Defences
Rule 13.6(3) is the biggest exception to the general Rule⁵
that a blanket denial will sufﬁce
for a statement of defence, or statement of defence to counterclaim. This subrule is commonly
overlooked, especially when a defendant ﬁles a defence to a claim in debt. That is serious if the
plaintiff moves for summary judgment, for three reasons. First an unpleaded defence is no bar to
summary judgment in favor of the plaintiff, even if the facts to support it are sworn to.⁶
Second, it is
arguable that some or all of R.13.6 invalidates a pleading which violates them. Third, a pleading
violating these Rules is most unconvincing; it sounds like a mere stall where there is no real
defence.
An employee cannot sue for wrongful dismissal if he unequivocally abandoned his contract
of employment, e. g. by ceasing to come to work. But the defendant must plead that, as it comes
within R.13.6(2) (b), and R.13.6(3). If the defendant does not plead it, it must move to amend.⁷
Many examples of how much detail to plead, and which statutes or similar defences must be
pleaded, are found in the Civil Procedure Encyclopedia.⁸
Lack of intent to create legal obligations
by an agreement must be pleaded.⁹
Settlement or release must be pleaded.¹⁰

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.6(4)
Rule 13.6(3) (r) does not require one to plead an obvious and fairly-well known section in a
public statute.¹
It should not surprise anyone. Aside from limitation and certain other statutes
named in this Rule, a normal Alberta statute found in the Revised Statutes need not be pleaded,
so long as the necessary facts are exactly pleaded.²
Reciting the Sale of Goods Act’s name and
chapter number, for example, implies ignorance on everyone’s part. A private Act, or one not in
the Revised Statutes, or an old English statute, should be pleaded. A statutory defence which was
neither raised nor pleaded below cannot be ﬁrst raised in the Supreme Court of Canada.³
Some questions between the plaintiff and the defendant do not arise unless the defendant
pleads them, such as identiﬁcation of a passenger with his driver’s negligence where there is no
gross negligence. Therefore, if the defendant does not plead that against the plaintiff, he may raise
it by third party notice.⁴
One need not plead a defence of set-off in order to establish equitable set-off, because the
language of Rule 3.59 is permissive and Rule 13.6(3), does not list set-off.⁵
I. Pleading Damages
Rule 13.6(2) (c) is one reason not to let a plaintiff seek damages for the ﬁrst time before the
appeal court.⁶
Beyond the bare prayer, one need not plead facts to show how one arrived at the general
damages claimed. But the common law requires that one plead facts to support any claim of
special damages. The boundary between special and general damages is sometimes difﬁcult,
as it involves two or more different tests. Any expense not yet incurred is probably general, not
special. So one probably need not separate claims for pain and suffering from those for future
wage loss, though it is customary to do so.⁷
One can get damages in lieu of speciﬁc performance, or for restoration or other equitable
damages, merely by claiming “damages”, without seeking speciﬁc performance or those other
types of damages.⁸
The prayer for relief sets a ceiling on the damages which the court may allow, only if the court
cannot or will not amend the prayer, and an award of more may be an implicit amendment.⁹
In the
Campbell Est.¹⁰
case, the trial judge should have been asked to amend the prayer, but failure to
do so was not fatal.
A claim for punitive or exemplary damages should be expressed in the Statement of Claim,
to give fair notice to the defendant. And the facts said to justify such damages should be pleaded
with some particularity.¹¹
A number of stray references in the facts in the Statement of Claim in one

J. Limitations Defences
Recent Notices to the Profession on the King’s Bench website, and minutes of a December

Footnote
9 2022 meeting with the Bar, suggest limitations problems set out below. Naming conventions
on electronic ﬁling matter a lot. Different clerks do different work or handle different types of
document. A poor name can lead to signiﬁcant delay. At one time, if a limitation period was close,
the lawyer or a trusted student or paralegal would personally go to the Clerk’s counter, get the
document ﬁled and paid for, and come back with the document bearing a ﬁling stamp dated within
the time limit. Now that is impossible for lawyers. Filing at the counter is forbidden then; it must be
done electronically. There is no plan to restore in-person ﬁling. And at one time, if the Clerk was
refusing a document for valid reasons, such as some information omitted, that could be quickly
ﬁxed on the spot, with a ballpoint pen. Or by seeing a Master immediately and getting an ex parte
ﬁat. None of that is possible now. There are two ways to ﬁle electronically in King’s Bench. One is
by the Filing Digital Service site. Whether it will give a lawyer a written acknowledgment of ﬁling
the same day is not stated. But only certain types of document can be ﬁled on that site. For other
types of document, or if one is not fully able to use the “FDS” system, one can ﬁle electronically
via email. But a Notice to the Profession stresses that such email ﬁling will probably not be dated
the day that it is received. If the clerk’s ofﬁce takes some days to process the document, it will
NOT be backdated to the day of receipt. So a perfectly proper document sent in two days before
the limitation expired, could well be shown as ﬁled some days after the limitation period expired.
(Filing often takes 2 or 3 days, in some places even 7. ). If you realize that you are nearing a
deadline, you can also ﬁle a special request to ensure that the deadline will not be missed. But
there are a number of ifs, ands, and buts attached to that process. This urgent procedure can only
be used with email ﬁling, NOT with the regular F. D. S. method of electronic ﬁling. See the King’s
Bench website under “Announcements/New Email-ﬁling-procedure”, for an exact procedure. Note
that the Clerks seem to regard 4: 00 as closing time. That is especially true for anything needing a
certiﬁcate of lis pendens and its ﬁling in the Land Titles system, as is common with builders’ liens.
The serious problems at Land Titles make it even harder to get a certiﬁcate of lis pendens. They
are a double set of mineﬁelds. The deadlines which electronic ﬁling can make difﬁcult to meet are
not just limitation periods. They can be court-imposed deadlines for complying with some order or
conditions for relief, some of which orders also impose automatic penalties such as striking out
or costs.

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.6(4)
On when the limitation period ends in most cases, see R.13.15.
A limitations statute does not automatically end a suit, and (especially because of subrule
(3) ’s paragraph (q) ), must be expressly pleaded.¹
A defendant gets no beneﬁt of an unpleaded
limitation period, especially where the Limitations Act expressly requires pleading it (as with s.11
on suits on judgments).²
If a defendant does not ﬁle a statement of defence, or if he ﬁles one but it
is struck out, then he cannot say that the limitation period still exists to bar the lawsuit.³
It is not sufﬁcient merely to plead matters mentioned in R.13.6. One must also plead the
facts giving rise to them: e. g. , “If the defendant broke the contract, which is denied, that breach
occurred more than x years before the action, which is barred by the Limitations Act. ”⁴
Even if the defendant has pleaded it, a limitation period is no ground to strike out a statement
of claim.⁵
The need to plead a limitation period does not prevent a defendant or third party from
moving (before ﬁling a defence) for summary judgment based on a limitation period.⁶
The Limitations Act and R.13.6(3) require that that statute be pleaded to be relied upon.
But if the proceeding is started by an originating notice (e. g. for judicial review), the respondent
ordinarily ﬁles no pleading or similar document, and is not permitted to ﬁle one, only later to give
argument. So the requirement of pleading must be met by giving written notice of the claim of
a limitation period, and an application under R.3.12 to permit ﬁling a defence to the originating
application. Or maybe a motion for leave to consider the Originating Notice to be a Statement of
Claim.⁷
On limitations defences, also see Part D above.
K. Miscellaneous
On the precise meaning of “scandalous”, “vexatious”, and “frivolous” in pleadings and how a
necessary plea for an arguable cause of action or defence cannot be any of these, see the Solgi
case.⁸
One of the topics which should be pleaded, to avoid surprise, is partnership or agency?⁹
Admissions in a pleading are stronger than evidence, and if there is no motion to withdraw
them, the trial judge cannot disregard them.¹⁰

R.13.7 PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
One cannot deliberately plead diametrically inconsistent and irreconcilable facts in different
actions, unless they are framed in the alternative.¹
But a statement of claim issued by accused’s
lawyer about an auto accident was not admissible against the accused in a criminal prosecution
over the same accident, as unduly prejudicial. Though it is a court document bearing the court’s
seal, that does not make it proof of the truth of its contents. Especially as the signiﬁcance could
not be foreseen at the time of its issue.²
A pleading was allowed in evidence in a later lawsuit
involving the original people, but with little relevant discussion.³
Defendants sued together may be separately represented (without costs penalties) where the
plaintiff pleaded serious charges of wrongdoing.⁴
The word “and/or” should not be used in a pleading.⁵
On what are pleadings for language laws purposes, see the Charlebois case.⁶
A statement of claim can be treated as a demand for payment, and so it is often not premature
for want of a previous demand.⁷
A creditor may have standing to sue in many situations, but the federal government is not a
creditor for taxes until there is an ofﬁcial tax assessment.⁸
Pleadings: Other Requirements
13.7 A pleading must give particulars of any of the following matters that are
included in the pleading:
(a) breach of trust;
(b) fraud;
(c) misrepresentation;
(d) wilful default;
(e) undue inﬂuence;
(f) defamation.⁹

13-39
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.7
A. Aims of Particulars vs. Fallacies
The usual purposes of particulars in pleadings are to give notice and prevent surprise, to show
what evidence to prepare to restrict claims and issues, and to bar going on to other matters.¹
Particulars are needed to tell the defendant the case to meet, let it prepare a defence, prevent
surprise at trial, and narrow the issues.²
More often R.13.7 is used as a procedural mechanism
where striking a claim, amending pleadings, or getting further particulars, is sought. Less often
is it used at trial to defeat a claim outright, especially if the missing details become known before
trial.³
Particulars are part of pleading, not discovery, not to get facts or evidence of them, but to clarify
or narrow allegations.⁴
Still less is it to get mixed facts and law.⁵
They are additional allegations by
the party pleading, usually narrowing the scope of the issues and what facts are relevant.
Therefore, the duty to give particulars does not depend upon what is the truth, nor what
a given party knows or does not know.⁶
It is no answer to a demand for particulars that the
defendant should know details of its own misconduct, because that assumes that the defendant
is guilty. Also, the defendant is entitled to know which transaction the plaintiff complains about.⁷
After all, if the defendant knows that he did nothing wrong, the bald allegation of the plaintiff is
mysterious and may cover the defendant’s entire life.⁸
The plaintiff knows more about the claim
than does the defendant.
One should not plead evidence, i. e. , how one will prove something.⁹
So particulars which are
evidence are not necessary or compellable.¹⁰
B. General Duty About Particulars
1. Particulars Required by a Rule
Rule 13.7 says that one must give particulars (with dates and items) of certain types of claim
or defence.¹¹
That is compulsory.¹²
For example, to plead fraudulent misrepresentation, a statement

of claim should give the gist of what the defendant stated to be true, and the intent that the plaintiff
act on it, and that he did, and with what result.¹
It should state what the truth was in fact, and state
that the defendant knew what he said was untrue, or that he was reckless whether it was true.
And it should state when and where the fraudulent statement was made and how (in a letter, by
telephone call, etc. ), and by whom (the defendant personally, or its manager Mr. Jones, etc. ).²
Where the statement of claim alleges fraud against a group, each defendant is entitled to know
what role of his is alleged: “What did I do?”³
But no speciﬁc word such as “fraud” or “dishonest” is
vital.⁴
Rule 13.7 also applies to a plea of injurious falsehood.⁵
Much the same is true of malicious prosecution.⁶
Likely the same is true of breach of a duty of
good faith.⁷
Or maybe wrongfully taking minerals?⁸
Rule 13.7 likely applies to a plea of illegality
too.⁹
Rule 13.7 means that undue inﬂuence, or the need for independent legal advice, must be
pleaded, even in a suit between solicitor and client.¹⁰
But not much speciﬁcity is required in a
statement of claim for breach of partnership ﬁduciary duties and misappropriation of funds?¹¹
A
statement of claim relying on malice by the defendant must do more than allege malice; it must
describe the facts that (if proven) would establish such claims. Facts must be pleaded to support
the plea of malice, just as would be the case with bad faith.¹²
2. Other Particulars Situations
It is in the nature of a conspiracy, that the defendants have secretly worked together, so likely
the plaintiff will not know what communications occurred, when, or with what content. The plaintiff
has to ﬁsh somewhat for conspiracy or breach of conﬁdence which are to a degree an exception.¹³

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.7
If a statement of claim pleads collective rights and says that a First Nation (not a party to
the suit) authorizes the claim, then the nature of the claim is unclear. If the rights claimed are
exercised by individuals for themselves, of a collective right, particulars are needed to say that. If
standing then becomes an issue, semble that is not for this stage. But if breach of ﬁduciary duty is
claimed, the acts constituting the breach are not enough; one needs particulars of the nature and
scope of that duty: how it arose, and what obligations resulted, and just how they were breached.¹
It is customary to give particulars of negligence, and ordinarily the pleadings (statement of
claim and statement of defence) would be largely useless if that is not done.²
Decisions by courts about how to resolve disputes over particulars in pleadings are highly
discretionary, and must be based on practicality and regard for the speciﬁc purposes of particulars,
the importance of which will differ from case to case. The main purposes are to allow the other
side to frame a defence, and to narrow the issues for trial and preparation for trial.³
Ordering
particulars after the pleadings have closed is to be done sparingly.⁴
One cannot be too rigid on
whether discovery is an adequate substitute for particulars, as it depends on the particular lawsuit
and its complexity.⁵
If pleadings are closed and discoveries are well underway, it is probably better
to let those pre-trial steps run their course before ruling on requests for particulars.⁶
It is common for a party to plead limited particulars, and then after some discovery, to learn
more and wish to add to the particulars previously stated.⁷
C. Format
When a party gives particulars at the time she ﬁles her pleading, they are normally incorporated
into the body of the pleading, often introduced by a statement such as “particulars of which
negligence include (a). . . (b). . . (c). . . , ” etc. If further particulars of an issued statement of claim
are sought, usually the new particulars of the statement of claim are not called an amendment
(though they could be). Usually the plaintiff would ﬁle a new document called “PARTICULARS”,
which begins like this: “1. Further particulars of paragraphs 7 and 14 of the statement of claim
include (a). . . (b). . . (c). . . ” etc.
D. Defamation Suits
Pleading in defamation is particularly strict and even stereotyped. A defamation statement of
claim must plead the defamation speciﬁcally, probably giving the exact words complained of.⁸
A
defence to a defamation suit must particularize the facts if it pleads truth, and there can be no
questioning (examination for discovery) on such facts without such a plea.⁹
It is especially important
that the pleadings in a defamation action be speciﬁc. It must set out the words complained of,

the time, place and manner of publication, to whom the publication was made,¹
