E. Speciﬁc Cases in R.13.7
Rule 13.7 is often overlooked.³
When it is breached, the opposing party is entitled to further
particulars without afﬁdavit, since the ﬂaw is evident on the face of the offending pleading.⁴
On
appeal, it is hard to ﬁnd fraud which differs in detail from what is pleaded, because of R.13.7.⁵
A
trial judge cannot leave to the jury fraud issues neither pleaded nor argued.⁶
But it is doubtful that R.13.7 allows the court to strike out the offending statement of claim.⁷
At trial a claim of fraud may fail if it was not pleaded or enough particulars given.⁸
F. Miscellaneous
Whether an application for particulars needs evidence to support it, depends upon whether
the gap or ﬂaw or lack or need in the pleading in question is plain on the face of that pleading.⁹
Particulars are usually more details about what has already been pleaded. But if they go
beyond the original statement of claim, then there may be a limitations defence, unless s.6(2) of
the Limitations Act applies. Calling something which is actually new, mere particulars, does not
make it so.¹⁰
A third request for particulars and the reply to it (the new particulars) were a proper
form of pleading and not an improper attempt to amend the pleadings, so discovery questions
based on those new particulars had to be answered.¹¹
The particulars need not include matters later arising during suit, and other unexpected
evidence may be admissible at trial.¹²
In the McLaughlin case¹³
the plaintiff won, but got no costs of the action because he pleaded
fraud but failed to prove it.

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.8(1)
In order to amend a pleading to add a plea for fraud, evidence is vital.¹
It is reasonable for a tribunal to exclude evidence until particulars of that topic are given, and if
the particulars are so late that the prejudice is irreparable, the evidence may never be admissible.²
Pleadings: Other Contents
13.8(1) A pleading may include all or any of the following:
(a) alternative claims or defences;³
(b) a statement of a point of law, and if so, the facts that make the point
of law applicable;⁴
(c) a matter that arose after the commencement document was ﬁled.⁵
(2) A general or other remedy claimed by a party need not be claimed in a
pleading.⁶

Related Provisions
13.13 (requirements for filed documents).

Usually it is not necessary to plead a point of law, but it is permissible to do so.⁷
The court should not strike out a statement of claim, even though against any one defendant it
sounds dubious, where the statement of claim claims in the alternative, and it is much more likely
that one of the defendants is liable.⁸
When an Alberta pleading alleges a list, such as a list of defendants, it impliedly includes as
an alternative that some or one of them did what is alleged; one reads such lists distributively.⁹
One pleading can contain inconsistent positions or allegations, if pleaded alternatively.¹⁰
“I
didn’t do it, and the plaintiff told me to do it” are good defences, if said to be alternative. Nor does
the plaintiff have to elect between alternative positions or relief until judgment is about to be
pronounced. So a plaintiff can sue for breach of contract, and claim rescission of the contract and

R.13.9 PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
speciﬁc enforcement in the alternative. When the judge ﬁnds the breach proved at the end of trial,
if in law either remedy is open, he or she will then ask the plaintiff to elect which remedy he wants,
since the two are opposites.¹
Two successive statements of claim cannot contradict each other?²
A statement of claim starts an action, and usually later events cannot give or add to the
plaintiff’s cause of action.³
But R.13.8(1) (c) does not so restrict the defendant. She can plead a
defence which arose after action brought, such as a compromise of the claim.⁴
The defendant can
do that either in her original statement of defence, or by getting permission to amend a defence
already ﬁled. Therefore in certain circumstances R.13.8(1) (c) lets a judge vary a pronounced
order not yet entered.⁵
In a lawsuit over a ﬁre, a defence that the plaintiff could not sue unless the breaches were
wilful, brought a Reply by the plaintiff that the breaches were wilful. That was not an improper new
ground of claim or new cause of action. It was all about the events in relationship already pleaded,
and a mere further description. The Reply was permissible.⁶
One cannot use R.13.8 to amend the statement of claim to allege the defendant’s wrongdoing
during and in the suit itself, e. g. false afﬁdavits and objecting to document production.⁷
Possibly the 2010 Rules now require a statement of claim to contain a plea for costs, especially
costs under a contract for full indemnity.⁸
Material from another lawsuit is not evidence of the truth of its contents.⁹
Defence of Tender
13.9 Tender before action may not be pleaded as a defence in an action unless,
before the defence is ﬁled, the defendant pays into Court the amount alleged to
have been tendered.¹⁰

Related Provisions
4.30 (formal offers and tender) ; 13.6(3) (p) (pleading tender) ; 13.48 (paying money into court).

Information Note
The “other requirements” for pleadings are mainly contained in rule 13.6 [Pleadings: general

be amended to include the impugned material, and such Rules on amendments are harder for the
plaintiff to satisfy, e. g. where delay would cause the defendant prejudice, or where it is not fair to
speculate that the law could evolve in the plaintiff’s favour. Delaying a scheduled class-action trial
with a new lengthy complex issue is serious. Unjust enrichment is a broad concept, and a difﬁcult
type of unjust enrichment can be a new cause of action.²
Pleadings: Speciﬁc Requirements for Class Proceedings
13.11(1) The title of a proceeding under the Class Proceedings Act must include
the words “Brought under the Class Proceedings Act” immediately below the
listed parties
(a) if it is intended, when the proceeding starts, that a certiﬁcation order
will be sought under the Act, or
(b) if a certiﬁcation order is subsequently made in respect of the
proceeding under the Act.
(2) If a certiﬁcation order is refused in respect of the proceeding or the proceeding
is decertiﬁed, the words “Brought under the Class Proceedings Act” must not
be included in the title in any subsequent pleadings and documents ﬁled in the
proceeding.³

R.13.11(2) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
A. The Tests
1. Basic Criteria
Of course Alberta’s Class Proceedings Act¹
is necessary reading. Each criterion in its s.5
must be met to certify a suit as a class action, though the amount of evidence which the plaintiffs
need to satisfy each criterion is not large.²
Case law says there are four necessary (but not sufﬁcient) criteria for a class action.³
These

Footnote
4 (or 5) criteria are procedural, and the certiﬁcation stage is not to decide merits, except very
preliminarily and loosely.⁴
Cause of action at this stage is based on the pleadings, not on
evidence.⁵
And for the other criteria (necessary conditions), only some basis in fact is needed, a
low threshold.⁶

identically situate vis-à-vis the defendant,¹

There can be a common
issue even if the answer to the suit could vary from one member to another within the

another member. And the “common” question must advance the resolution of each
member’s claim.³
A substantial ingredient common to all members is necessary, but there

Must the class exclude any individuals who have no claim against
the defendant?⁵
The commonality test in Dutton (Alta) applies across Canada.⁶
It is not
necessary to establish that each class member suffered a loss.⁷
The “common success”
requirement simply means that success for one class member does not mean failure for
another.⁸
Success for one need not mean success for all. If too many of the “common”
issues proposed are too broad, a class action may not be the preferable procedure.⁹
If
the class are linked only by a common scheme by the defendant, there must be evidence
of the common scheme, not a mere need to identify each person and then see if his or
her personal circumstances and dealings were similar.¹⁰
Common issues can be proved
at the certiﬁcation stage by expert evidence which is sufﬁciently credible.¹¹
Charter claims
generally will not meet the commonality requirement.¹²

R.13.11(2) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
suits,¹
and also preferable to the other alternatives, including joinder, test cases, and
consolidation of lawsuits.²
Preferability should largely focus on economy, access to
justice, and modifying behaviour.³
Preferability includes whether individual issues would
predominate.⁴
To see whether a class action is the preferable procedure, one looks at all
the individual and common issues raised by the claims in this “factual matrix”. The question
then is whether the class proceeding presents a fair, efﬁcient and manageable way to
decide the common issues, in the light of the goals of judicial economy, access to justice,
and modifying behavior of wrongdoers.⁵
Preferability is easy when each class member
is likely to get a small award, but much harder if the named representative plaintiff holds
open future substantial claims, or if the action is complex with many different causes of
action.⁶
And “preferable” involves balancing the interests of all parties and the court, and
can involve the resources of individual class members, economics, number of issues,
complexities, and alternative means.⁷
No one factor is determinative.⁸
This is a high-level
macro consideration, weighing the pros and cons of the different possible types of legal
proceeding.⁹
If common issues on damages or causation are proposed, the plaintiff must
show (with evidence) a workable methodology to decide such issues on a class-wide
basis. Plaintiffs need a methodology (though not, at this stage, an amount), and a realistic
prospect of establishing a class wide loss.¹⁰
A remedy such as giving up proﬁts, is not a
cause of action, but it must be more advantageous than individual damages to individual
class members.¹¹
Plaintiffs wishing to have their class suit certiﬁed must meet all ﬁve requirements in s.5(1), (2)
of the Act,¹²
and when they do, certiﬁcation is mandatory.¹³
The factors relevant to certiﬁcation in the Class Actions Act are expressly not a closed list,
and the new element of proportionality is also relevant and applicable. This is a new culture shift,
emphasizing new procedures.¹⁴

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.11(2)
Whether a class action is the preferable procedure is more important than whether the action
is likely to succeed; the latter is not the question at the certiﬁcation stage.¹
Though the best minds have attacked this problem, such suits still present uncertainty and
procedural wrangling.²
Alberta inserts the problem into the criteria for certifying a class action (s.
5) and in one suit, the case management Judge adequately assessed those factors.³
2. Other Relevant Factors
An underlying aim of class actions is avoiding multiplicity of lawsuits, but that is not always
prohibited, and the right of each injured person to bring and prosecute his or her own separate
lawsuit cannot be barred.⁴
Even if the plaintiffs satisfy all the necessary criteria, other factors may weigh in exercising
the court’s discretion against a class suit: different defences against different plaintiffs, need for
separate questioning (examinations for discovery, ) important non-common issues, or a small
class (so each member should be a plaintiff),⁵
and need for actual reliance of individual plaintiffs.
Even if determining the common questions does not in each case advance the claims of both
the classes, sometimes the factual matrix underlying the ﬁndings on each common question will
be the same for each class, so that is the desirable method. A ﬁnding against class members on
the common issues will remove any need to make individual claims, thus achieving much judicial
economy. The broader the classes, ordinarily the greater the preferability of class proceedings.
Assessing the causes of action is part of the certiﬁcation analysis, the factual matrix raises other
broader considerations too, including judicial economy and access to justice.⁶
Certain conditions are irrelevant and no bar to a class suit: some need for individual assessment
of damages, different contracts, different remedies, or subgroups with separate issues. Efﬁciency
and fairness must be balanced in the decision.⁷
3. The Pleadings
At the certiﬁcation stage, the pleadings are construed generously and holistically.⁸
And low quality of the pleadings, curable by amendment, may have some weight.⁹
For each of the facts required by s.5 of the Act for certiﬁcation, the court must be satisﬁed,
and pleadings do not sufﬁce.¹⁰

(Alta. ) 2017 ABCA 284, Edm 1603 0177 AC (Sep 7), varg 11/18 Jul ’16 (¶ 29).
2 Ravvin v. Cdn. Bread Co.2020 ABCA 424, [2021] 4 WWR 1 (¶’s 30-35), leave den (SCC 20 May ’21).

R.13.11(2) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
4. Merits of the Suit
The court will not certify a class action if the claim is not arguable on the merits.¹
The Class
Proceedings Act does not authorize non-existent or unarguable claims, even if they are mixed in
with better arguable claims.²
At the certiﬁcation stage of a class action, any evidence can be afﬁdavits on information and
belief, and business documents can be relied upon. Before discovery, plaintiffs often cannot get
personal-knowledge evidence.³
But that may not be necessary, as the facts stated in the statement
of claim are presumed to be true.⁴
Expert evidence should not be reviewed at this stage.⁵
At the certiﬁcation stage of a class action, the judge’s role is just to ﬁlter out frivolous claims,
nothing more. So an arguable case alleged sufﬁces. If the allegations are precise enough, they
are assumed to be true.⁶
It was expressed as “some basis in fact”.⁷
A possible defence to a suit is irrelevant at the certiﬁcation stage.⁸
The Class Proceedings Act emphasizes procedural topics: only a weak level of fact⁹
and
merits must be shown to certify; the test is similar to that for striking out (or not) a statement of
claim.¹⁰
The chambers judge does not really look at the merits.¹¹
The evidence of harm to one or
more class members need be neither ﬁrst hand, nor strong.¹²
The court assumes the facts pleaded
and documents pleaded are assured to be true, and the court takes judicial notice of the law.¹³
But
pleas of misrepresentation without particulars may not sufﬁce.¹⁴
Some cases refuse certiﬁcation
on the ground that the common cause of action suggested is not arguable.¹⁵
A forbidden “merits test” (whether the suit would succeed), is not the only possible liability or
damages test. Also relevant is whether even if the class suit succeeded for baseline or nominal

damages, the likely recovery would be de minimis, even negative, and so contrary to judicial
economy and a class action not needed for access to justice.¹
The usefulness of trying a common issue is not removed by some doubts as to whether one
certain class has a legal interest in the outcome of certain common issues.²
But a suit for pure economic loss needs a proximate relationship, which requires some
undertaking by the defendant, especially as environmental legislation does not usually create
new heads of tort.³
But otherwise loss or damage need not be proven at the certiﬁcation stage,
though the statement of claim should brieﬂy describe the type of loss (class wide).⁴
5. Practical Rules of Thumb
If you have doubt about applicability of these Rules and the Supreme Court of Canada’s
criteria⁵
to your circumstances, you should ask yourself questions like these ones:
(a) Is there a clear arguable cause of action between the members of the class and the
defendant?⁶
(b) Is the class capable of easy and deﬁnite objective ascertainment?
(c) Are individual determinations of awards necessary?⁷
(d) Does the cause of action depend on individual circumstances (e. g. , misrepresentation) ?
Or instead, will success for one mean success or signiﬁcant progress for all?
(e) Has provision been made for questioning (discovery) or costs?
(f) Is there a plan for dealing with or notifying individuals who may be unwilling to be part of
the representative action, or whose claims might be affected (e. g. , by res judicata) ?
(g) Will disallowance of the action in representative form give rise to limitation problems for
individual members of the class later on?
6. Conﬂicts in the Class
It is not necessary for the plaintiff’s expert to establish that each and every member suffered
a loss. The principle of “common issues” is whether allowing the case to proceed as a class
proceeding will avoid duplication of fact ﬁnding or legal analysis.⁸
But a known pipeline rupture

R.13.11(2) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
and spill does not justify a class of everyone living in a 1500-square-kilometre area, including
three towns and multiple water bodies not directly affected. There must be a rational relation
and “colorable claim” of each person, especially as no person living near the one river and its
downstream branches had come forward.¹
If the wronged plaintiffs want different remedies, and face different time limits and interest, a
class suit may not be apt, and the court cannot force on the would-be plaintiff a different class
than the one he proposes.
In a proposed class action if there is a sub-class, some individual factual and legal analysis
for them is not a necessity or a certainty at this early stage, if no subclass common issues were
proposed for that subclass. That can be worked out later by the common issues trial judge, and
need not be worked out at the outset.²
If the representative plaintiff has in the same suit an individual claim not part of the common
issues, nor based on the same facts, that plaintiff and his counsel may well have a conﬂict of
interest, so one law ﬁrm cannot represent both.³
Class size alone is not a reason to deny certiﬁcation, but a large size makes it far more likely
that the class is over-inclusive and contains members who have no tenable claim against the
defendant.⁴
B. Opting In or Out of Suit
See also Part G below.
Section 17 of the Class Proceedings Act has been rewritten.⁵
A person who opts out of the
class has a right to be converted instead into an individual named plaintiff.
Though the law discourages multiplicity of suits, the right of an individual harmed to commence
and prosecute his or her own separate lawsuit is almost always inviolable.⁶
Where most of the group have joined and funded the suit, the court may decide to include only
those others who elect (after notice) to join.⁷
All potential class members should get notice of an
application. Non-common issues may have to be decided in individual proceedings, and need for
actual reliance of individual plaintiffs does not bar a class action.⁸
If there is a case management judge, only that judge can hear a motion to opt out of the class
action.⁹

Where someone wanted to certify and at once settle a 13-member class action, but one
member strenuously dissented, the latter was allowed to opt out, and almost none of his own
existing suit was stayed, even though the defendant would not settle the class action without the
dissenter; the dissenter’s position was different, and co-opting him would be unfair.¹
An Alberta suit was given an interim stay to allow its class a chance to opt into a parallel
Saskatchewan class action, which was intended to be nationwide. The court discusses opting in
or out, and which is default mode under different provinces’ legislation and their relative merits.
Few class members opt at all. The right to sue in one’s home province should not be removed.
Different courts should cooperate. Not all parallel proceedings in different provinces are abusive.²
If someone starts his own individual lawsuit, and then opts out of a similar class action, his
opting out makes it possible for his separate suit to continue, but the court may even so temporarily
stay his individual suit if the unique individual issues and the class issues cannot be separated.
Various other factors relevant to a temporary stay are discussed.³
C. Court Permission for Class Action
No court leave is required to commence a proposed class action,⁴
though one can probably
count on a defendant’s challenging it once started. Individual members of the group are jointly and
severally liable for costs and, arguably, subject to questioning (discovery) as persons for whose
beneﬁt the action is brought.⁵
But after commencement, the proposed representative plaintiff must then apply to the court
to have the suit certiﬁed as a class action.
Certiﬁcation motions require balancing often conﬂicting considerations, many of them
practical.⁶
Decisions about certiﬁcation are procedural; they stand unless and until a material change of
circumstances is shown.⁷
D. Examples
A class action was not proper in a nuisance suit, because the common issues were a small
fraction of the issues. And resolving them would not speed up resolving the others.⁸
Class action suits have been approved in product liability cases,⁹
for all victims of the same
fraudulent scheme with some common elements,¹⁰
or for students at a residential school.¹¹
Not all of the class had a cause of action, a number of subclasses would be a less preferable
procedure, common issues were too broadly and vaguely deﬁned, unrelated claims for unrelated

events against different defendants were lumped together, and the named plaintiff had a conﬂict
of interest. The action was not certiﬁed.¹
In the ﬁrst time periods, the legal and factual questions affecting individual class members
would predominate over common questions. And the legal issues for time period would be very
different than those for a period where certiﬁcation was proper.²
The nature of the remedy (e. g. , damages) or the nature of the cause of action (e. g. ,
misrepresentation) may in fact make some such claims difﬁcult to mount.³
A suit by an oil company against the Crown for overpayment of royalties under a lease on an
Indian reserve is not a use of R.12.1, and the Crown is not sued as trustee, and this is not a claim
against the trust property.⁴
But a shareholder of a closely-held company can sue as the trustee
of the company?⁵
A suit alleging sexual abuse involves intent of the actors, not carelessness or poor operation
of an institution. So duty of care is not the issue, and it does not sufﬁce unless there were actual
assaults. But if there were assaults, the bodies and the assaulting employee would quite possibly
be jointly liable either vicariously for their employee, or directly if they had a duty to prevent such
events. It is not necessary that a common issue result in a ﬁnal determination of liability, only that
it would advance the action though some individual issues would remain to try.⁶
E. Legal Fees
Only Alberta’s Class Actions legislation requires advance approval of a contingency fee
agreement. Notice should go to the Public Trustee if many involved in the class would not be sui
juris. Notice would ordinarily not be given to the defendant.
There was a lengthy discussion of what should be the criteria for fees’ approval.⁷
25% of
recovery is within the fee range usually negotiated in Alberta for contingency agreements,
particularly as some of the claims would not involve any recovery even if they were completely
successful in law.⁸
F. Procedure
1. Evidence
On evidence, see also Parts A.3 and A.4 above.
A plaintiff could validly serve a Notice to Admit in a suit brought under the class proceedings
legislation, even before the certiﬁcation motion or a statement of defence. Nor should the court
without more defer the time for replying to that Notice.⁹

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.11(2)
What procedure is preferable is largely a question of law, so afﬁdavits and cross-examination
are not necessary?¹
If there is a contested motion (with afﬁdavits) to certify a class action, what
is the correct sequence for receiving afﬁdavits from one side, cross-examining on them, and
receiving afﬁdavits from the other side? The decision is up to the case management judge. An
agreement by counsel gets deference. A party can do little useful without knowing what is the
case against him or her. Cross-examination before rebuttal evidence may sometimes save some
time and narrow the issues.²
So here the usual order of evidence and cross-examination on an
ordinary application, was applied to this certiﬁcation application.
Generally speaking, no questioning (discovery) is open pending a motion to certify a class
action.³
But the court can give directions to ﬁll in various procedural gaps or ﬂaws before the ﬁnal
decision on certiﬁcation.⁴
2. Regulating the Class Action
Usually the proposed plaintiff provides a litigation plan; the court evaluates it for adequacy,
but it need not be incapable of improvement.⁵
Counsel are needed in a class action, and self-
representation is not appropriate.⁶
If there are subclasses, speciﬁc procedures may be necessary.⁷
Whether pleadings should be closed, or at least the defences ﬁled, before a motion to certify
the class action is heard, depends on the circumstances and is very discretionary. The question is
not whether a defence is necessary, but whether it would be helpful. Here the defences would be
very helpful, and ﬁling them should not be deferred until after the certiﬁcation is decided.⁸
All necessary defendants should be added and amendments be made to the statement of
claim, before the suit is certiﬁed.⁹
It is not for the chambers judge hearing a motion to certify, to ﬁll
in gaps in the statement of claim.¹⁰
3. Other Applications
Pending the plaintiff’s motion to certify its statement of claim as a class action, the defendant
can move summarily to dismiss the suit, e. g. as sued outside the limitation period. Which motion is

The Class Proceedings Act says “the Court”, so an Applications

4. Later Changes
There is a broad power to amend a certiﬁcation order, thus narrowing the scope of res
judicata.⁹
A request by another party, not the counsel concerned, to change the law ﬁrm representing
the named plaintiff involves looking at counsel’s competence, motives for the request, and any
prejudice to the class, but is not a test of relative ability of two ﬁrms.¹⁰
If the request is because the
same lawyer has switched law ﬁrms he or she has no independent status, nor does either ﬁrm,
but it was allowed absent prejudice or bad motive.¹¹
There are strict tests for severing and trying ﬁrst an issue. The plaintiffs did not suggest that a
split here would likely dispose of all or any part of their claims, merely that it would assist records

G. Competing and Multi-Jurisdiction Litigation
See also Part B above.
Generally on geographic jurisdiction over class actions, see Lapointe v. Cassels Brock case.⁴
The Court of King’s Bench enacted a Civil Practice Note,⁵
effective September 4, 2018. It
adopted the Canadian Bar Association’s Class Action Judicial Protocol of 2018.
Alberta Rules are much more explicit on what are the criteria for a multi-jurisdiction class
action, than are the Rules anywhere else. There had been little judicial consideration of these
Rules. They were analyzed and applied.⁶
It is harder to show that a multi-province class action is the preferable procedure if the governing
legislation is provincial, and their wording varies signiﬁcantly between provinces; combined with
varying factual or contractual situations for different members of the class, this could become too
complex and unhelpful to be the preferable route. And opinions or desires of class members could
differ between provinces.⁷
Class action legislation is presumed not to allow a superior court to hear a suit which would
otherwise come within the jurisdiction of another court or tribunal, because of its subject matter.⁸
Staying an individual lawsuit because someone is bringing a class action whose class would
include that private suit, is often not a sufﬁcient reason to stay the ﬁrst private one. One must use
the usual 3-part test for stays, and show speciﬁc risk of harm to the persons seeking the stay

Individuals have a right to sue alone for wrongs to them, and it takes compelling reasons to
interfere with that right, and then only to the extent necessary to protect competing rights.¹
An
Alberta class action began about 7 weeks after a similar Ontario action but before second and third
Ontario class actions. Alberta would not stay its suit, which was further advanced.²
Overlapping
and parallel class actions in different jurisdictions are not necessarily abusive or vexatious, but
can become abusive, either through long delay (and thus evasion of many limitation periods), or
through unfair tactics or waste of court resources.³
“Multi-jurisdictional” class actions are not always improper. But duplicative class actions on
the same topic for no legitimate purpose, undermine the aims of economy, access, and modifying
behavior. Settlement and enforcement of judgments becomes more difﬁcult. The defendant can
pick the suit where it can get the cheapest settlement. The lack of a national coordinated approach
is the main difﬁculty.⁴
Redundant or heavily overlapping causes of action cause big problems of manageability, even
if each is properly pleaded and arguable and so could not be struck out. If deciding one will
answer the key issue common to everyone in the class, there is no point to certifying more than
one of those issues pleaded. If several provinces are covered and the legislation differs in them,
it may be necessary to plead and certify several of the causes of action.⁵
Counsel for the representative plaintiff in a national class proceeding has a duty to give
evidence applicable for each provincial or regional market involved. The courts should demand
this, and if it is not done, then a stay of other suits in other provinces may be reopened. And the
court selected for a national suit has to take into account those interests outside that province.⁶
Though one plaintiff should not have two similar suits overlapping, a proposed class action
not yet certiﬁed is no bar to an individual suit, especially if a limitation period looms.⁷
If a case
management judge strikes the representative (class) parts of a statement of claim, but years later,
the Court of Appeal ﬁnds error and sends it back to rehear, the case management judge may well
be better suited than it to decide on the merits.⁸
When a class proceeding covering the topic of
another lawsuit is certiﬁed, the effect of certiﬁcation is in effect to stay any action within that class.⁹
Class action legislation is to be favoured, and even if it is “only procedural”, that does not make
it less worthy of protection than an arbitration clause in a contract. It seems to be implied that even
a clear arbitration clause cannot prevail against it?¹⁰
In class actions with pan-Canadian scope, the responsible judges from several provinces can
sit together to decide common issues, all sitting outside their home provinces, without video hook-
up with the home province, so long as they exercise no coercive powers.¹¹

13-61
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.11(2)
That one suit is a class action introduces other considerations for staying one of two similar
suits, found in the aims of class actions. Yet individual litigants have the right to autonomy. An all-
Alberta class action expressly lets the case management judge stay or sever related proceedings
(Class Proceedings Act s.14), which gives a broad discretion. In such cases whether the individual
litigant is a member of the class and whether he or she opted out, is important. If an Ontario
class action has given ﬁnal judgment and the present Alberta parties belong to at least one of
its classes, they cannot relitigate the Ontario certiﬁed common issues. The issue is not whether
the Ontario judge binds judges elsewhere; it is whether he or she binds the litigants, including all
class members. A litigant should not recover double compensation, so different legal bases for the
same loss do not dictate separate repeated litigation about damages or remedy.¹
Saskatchewan has 2007 amendments to its class actions legislation, from the Uniform Law
Conference’s 2005 report, to deal with overlapping class actions in different provinces. The
amendment allows for a multi-party class action but allows those residing outside Saskatchewan
to opt out. The legislation does not extend Saskatchewan’s jurisdiction, and merely limits
overlapping. The tests are discussed. A big issue is whether to recognize an existing Ontario
order for a multi-province class action.²
H. Party-Party Costs
The situation respecting costs of a failed suit, and the legislative and other history of that topic,
are reviewed.³
Persistent unsuccessful objections to approving settlement of a large class action, in ﬁve
provinces, with appeals, led to triple Column 5 cost payable by those opposing, who were
commercial entities protecting their own private interests.⁴
The general costs Rules apply to class actions.⁵
And ﬁnancial beneﬁts to the plaintiffs bar a
claim to be litigating to beneﬁt society, a private action could have settled the law, and behavior
modiﬁcation is irrelevant if there was no illegality.⁶
Though some provinces bar costs in a class action,⁷
Alberta’s Legislature has chosen the
opposite approach. It was proper for the chambers judge who certiﬁed the action to refuse to rule
in advance on whether costs would be possible and to leave it until later in the lawsuit.⁸
Even in
a class action, costs are discretionary and hard to upset on appeal. Class actions can be very

expensive. The plaintiffs and their lawyers here would have reaped huge proﬁts, had they won.
Statutory permission to waive costs against the plaintiffs does not bar making them pay costs
when the Court dismissed the suit on the merits.¹
Behavior modiﬁcation is a legitimate consideration for party-party costs, especially in a class
action. That the suit is a class action is itself also relevant.²
Deep pockets are usually irrelevant,
but have some weight in a class action.³
Not all class actions’ certiﬁcation applications attract enhanced costs (e. g. a multiple of
Schedule C). That there were extra steps can be dealt with by giving costs for each step.⁴
Stay of an individual lawsuit in the context of parallel class action proceedings was at the time
novel, and the decision here would guide others. That was one ground to let each party bear its
own costs.⁵
I. Settlements
To approve settlement or not, the court decides whether the proposed settlement is “fair,
reasonable and in the best interests of the class”.⁶
Of course the settlement should release only the claims certiﬁed (and pleaded) in this present
class action. It must not cover the subject of other suits.⁷
The basic scheme of the Act is to let a few representative plaintiffs sue for and settle for, all
class members, albeit with some court supervision, and if any dissent can prevail, especially late,
the scheme cannot work.⁸
Class action settlements should be viewed with suspicion and seriously scrutinized. The court
should investigate all aspects of the proposed settlement and evidence before approval. When
reviewing an “arm’s length” settlement negotiation, the court moves to a presumption of fairness,
absent any evidence to the contrary.⁹
Courts must be careful in approving settlements of class actions, as a party may have interests
diverging somewhat from those of the class. But evidence is needed to conclude that a party is
partial.¹⁰
One province’s decision about a class settlement does not bind the court of another
province, but it should get considerable weight. And if one party agrees to cooperate with its
former opponent in advancing its case in other provinces, that can have huge value. Relitigating
a long expensive matter in two provinces is undesirable.¹¹

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.11(2)
In order to promote settlement, if the counsel for the representative plaintiffs is not interested
in settling, the case management judge familiar with the history of the class action may
temporarily remove the counsel for the representative plaintiffs and appoint other class counsel,
the old counsel still being able to represent the representative plaintiffs and advise them. The
representative plaintiffs were not removed, and would have to approve any settlement.¹
Existing named plaintiffs wanted to add a further named plaintiff and had wished to change
counsel also, because of differences over settlement negotiations. The appellant existing plaintiffs
had since retained Ontario counsel experienced in such suits. There was reason to believe adding
a fourth plaintiff was the ﬁrst step toward removing the appellants as named plaintiffs. The motion
was arguable.²
When a class action is settled, commonly the court approves both the settlement and the fees
of counsel for the class, often at very large sums.³
Court approval is necessary, and depends on
whether the settlement is fair, reasonable, and in the best interests of those affected.⁴
Where there
is only a set of fees and disbursements for the representative plaintiff in a class action, and that
counsel appeared for the class across all the jurisdictions, it is appropriate to accede to the court
from the province in which counsel is located for approval of fees.⁵
Complex problems of the costs
for many parties arguing in a number of provinces about whether to approve a settlement of a
multi-province class action, are discussed and decided.⁶
On party-party costs of contested approval applications, see Part H above.
J. Miscellaneous
Two people sufﬁce for a class, and a class of 69 or fewer is ample, even if many members may
not want to participate.⁷
Plaintiffs cannot be forced to seek certiﬁcation of their suits as class actions, if the individual
plaintiffs have causes of action.⁸
If the named representative plaintiff and counsel are not ideal, the court may leave them in
place if better ones cannot easily be found.⁹
The standard of review on appeal from a chambers judge’s decision as to certiﬁcation are
described as deferential, absent enough error.¹⁰
OR as especially deferential.¹¹
Quebec law sets four preconditions for certifying a class action, and if the chambers judge errs
in law with respect to one of the criteria, or if his assessment of it is clearly wrong, the Court of
Appeal can substitute its own assessment of that criterion, but not of the other three.¹²

R.13.12(1) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
Pleadings: Denial of Facts
13.12(1) Every fact in a pleading is denied if the fact is not admitted in another
pleading ﬁled by a party opposite in interest.¹
(2) A denial of a fact in a pleading must meet the point of substance.²

Related Provisions
6.37 (notice to admit)

See also R.13.6 nn. A, C, D, E, H.
Generally speaking, a statement of defence must do one of 3 things: either deny some or all of
the plaintiff’s fact allegations, or say that the ones pleaded do not give a cause of action, or allege
further facts in addition to the ones pleaded.³
It can do two or more of those.⁴
Given this Rule, a denial by the defendant should be interpreted broadly.⁵
A pleading must not contain evasive responses on points of substance,⁶
and can be struck out
if it is very prolix, confusing, and inconsistent.
It is necessary for a defendant to plead speciﬁcally that someone else was to pay for the
work.⁷
Or that the deal was an investment agency, not a loan.⁸
Or that despite an agreement,
there was no intention to create legal obligations.⁹
Or that the work tendered did not conform to
speciﬁcations.¹⁰
See also Rr.13.6 and 13.12.
This Rule (and others) was violated by failure to plead non-survival of the cause of action and
a causation issue, and they could not be advanced after the close of evidence (and death of the
plaintiff).¹¹
The pleading must be read as a whole to decide if it amounts to an admission.¹²
If one party pleads some topic on which the opposing party’s privilege might be relevant, a
mere denial by the party with the privilege does not waive its privilege. A mere denial does not
change a party’s position in any respect. Indeed silence is deemed to be a denial: R.13.12(1). A
simple denial just means “Prove it”. It merely identifying what must be done.¹³

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.12(2)
CHECKLIST FOR STATEMENTS OF DEFENCE
A. Is it safe to seek a time extension for ﬁling the defence? (Rr.3.34(1), 3.33(3), 13.5(1) Or
would that estop one from later objecting, e. g.
1. to a limitation period now about to expire,
2. or that the wrong party had sued after a limitation period now about to expire?
B. Are there any applications which one should ﬁle before defending, because ﬁling a statement
of defence would
1. attorn to the jurisdiction of the Alberta court? or
2. waive procedural defects? (R.1.5(2) )
C. Parties
1. (a) Will this be a defence for all parties, or some only?
(b) Is there any conﬂict of interest among the defendants?
2. Are there any errors in the names of the parties in the style of cause?
3. Are there any trade names or partnerships? (Rr.2.2(1) -2.5(1), 13.6(3) (n), Partnership
Act)
4. Do any parties need someone’s approval to sue or be sued?
D. Contents of Defence
1. What to admit? (R.10.33(2) (b) )
2. General denial? (but cf. R.13.12(2) )
3. Does any contract or legislation require notice before the plaintiff’s suit? e. g. Defamation
Act or Municipal Government Act?
4. Plead expressly (R.13.6(3) )
(a) time limitations (contractual or statutory),
(b) Statute of Frauds, the Guarantees Acknowledgment Act, Dower Act, Law of Property
Act, or Unconscionable Transactions Act
(c) illegality or insufﬁciency of promise, contract or like
(d) estoppel, release, voluntary assumption of risk, waiver, lack of capacity (R.13.6(3) )
(e) matters which would surprise the plaintiff (R.13.6(3) )
5. (a) Must plead
(i) any different version of the transaction (including wrong party), or
(ii) positive averments, or
(iii) confession and avoidance (R.13.8)
(b) deny the plaintiff’s factual allegations in substance, not evasively (R.13.12(2) )
6. Plead exact facts needed to trigger any sections of any statutes relied on by the
defendants, e. g. Traffic Safety Act, Occupiers’ Liability Act, Proceedings Against the
Crown Act, Sale of Goods Act, Bills of Exchange Act, Securities Act.
7. If the Statement of Claim makes money claims, or claims on cheques or bills of exchange,
deny expressly some matter of fact on which the plaintiff bases the alleged liability
8. Plead points of law? (R.13.8) (but cf.6 above)
9. Be brief (R.13.6), and omit mere evidence.
10. Give particulars of breach of trust, fraud, misrepresentation, wilful default, undue
inﬂuence, and defamation (R.13.7).
11. If there is a defence of tender, pay the money in (R.13.9).

(2) Whether or not a form is prescribed, each document must begin with the
following:
(a) the name of the Court;
(b) the name of the judicial centre;

(d) the action number;

(i) anything required by these rules to be included.

the remainder being omitted and replaced by the words “and others”.
(5) Every document ﬁled and every exhibit to an afﬁdavit must be legible.
(6) When a document is ﬁled, the court clerk must retain the original of the
document.
(7) Authorities referred to in a document provided to the Court must be cited in
accordance with rule 14.25(1) (h).

The “News & Announcements” part of the Court of King’s Bench website contains an April
20, 2022 notice about new email filing procedures and naming conventions. It is long and
complex. There is another long detailed one of April 20, 2022 with a long title Beginning “Updated:
Announcements from Court and Justice Services, about filing” which is said to be different from
“the King’s Bench Filing Digital Service”. It says that which documents can be accepted by the
Court of King’s Bench Filing Digital Service are listed on https: //qb-filing. alberta. ca/. This April 20,

Footnote
2022 notice gives many “naming conventions” for emails and documents. Another announcement
of May 16, 2022 (effective June 15) says certain types of Surrogate grants may be applied for
digitally, using the online Surrogate Digital Service. A June 27, 2022 notice enacts a new “Filing
procedure [for] Judicial Review of SafeRoads Decisions”. Effective September 2, 2022, all Judicial
Centres offer Civil Filing Digital Service, which can be used by lawyers and their assistants for
many filings with no fee waiver. Effective November 1, it is mandatory for pleadings in the Court
of King’s Bench. An account is necessary. That notice is dated September 27, 2022. In the

extending the cap on email filing fees. And there is also found a July 8, 2020 set of guidelines for

Also on the Court of King’s Bench website is an April 2023 document headed “King’s Bench
Filing Digital Service - accepted document types” which lists what documents may or may not be
filed electronically and whether in their own right or to support or oppose an application of some
kind.
The King’s Bench has two services for ﬁling: the civil digital service (FDS) and its Court Case
Management Service (CCM) effective Dec.6 ’23 are extended. “. . . all CCM court document
ﬁling will be handled using FDS, with a wider array of documents now accepted through this

receivership matters, and ﬁlings by government lawyers”.¹

Digital Service expands Family Law Act document ﬁling to the JCE and the JCC. It gives a list of
document types which will be accepted. Someone new to this service must create an account.

alberta. ca/help. This service cannot be used if for that type of document, or that, litigant, no fee
is payable; then email must be used.²

subject, on which see R.6.2 n. A.2. A further Practice Note is promised by that Announcement of

The King’s Bench has announced that the Clerks will set the date of ﬁling of each document,
which date is always later than the date of receipt. One decision says the Court may change that
date to an earlier date, so long as it is on or after actual receipt. That can save a commencement
document from a later retroactive date and so retroactive cancellation by a limitation period.³

to one, see Rr.6.2, 6.3, and 6.9, and notes to them.

13-69
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.13(7)
Apart from an afﬁdavit, a factum, or a judgment, no court document requires an actual written
signature. And an electronic signature sufﬁces, so it is not necessary to order that facsimile or
electronic signatures will sufﬁce.¹
Legislation barring publication of names does not bar use of real names in pleadings or
judgments. The presumption is in favour of open courts, and the mere desire for privacy is not
enough to displace it. No permission was granted to sue in a pseudonym, as there was no evidence
that the real name’s use would cause harm.²
Where a party is publicly identiﬁed by initials or
pseudonym, and then costs are awarded, to enforce them the judgment creditor may get an order
for a writ in the proper name, without having to amend the style of cause; the Civil Enforcement
Act says so.³
A style of cause should not contain professional designations, honoriﬁcs, or other
descriptions of status or qualiﬁcation (unless the privilege is statutory).⁴
Co-plaintiffs must all have the same solicitor of record, unless the court gives leave for separate
solicitors.⁵
A lawyer in good standing elsewhere in Canada, and with appropriate insurance, may appear
in an Alberta court if he or she reveals to the court that he or she is not a member of the Alberta
Bar.⁶
Despite its title, a document ﬁled with the Clerk is not a statement of defence if it merely says
that the defendant will defend (or will counterclaim) and that he has no ﬁxed address, but tells
how to reach him. At best, it is a demand for notice.⁷
Counsel could not appear for a defendant who had ﬁled nothing, and should ﬁrst ﬁle a
statement of defence or Demand for Notice. Though counsel disagreed, that ruling by a judge
bound later judges.⁸
A judge can take notice of pleadings and orders on the court ﬁle.⁹
A purported copy of a
statement of claim and third party notices with Clerk’s rubber stamp, had no corresponding
court ﬁle or original documents, were not authorized by the person named as plaintiff, and were
declared not proper court documents.¹⁰
If a document’s sufﬁciency or validity is questioned, that the court ofﬁce ﬁled it is not important;
the Clerk or Registrar should err on the side of accepting papers and leave any irregularity for the

R.13.14(1) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES

There may be some power in the court to deem a document ﬁled on
the day the Clerk received it electronically, even if the Clerk deemed it ﬁled only some days later?
See Sabir v. Gill, R.3.1 n.
The Rules require an address for service of documents, and contact information (and name
and address) for the party ﬁling.²
And if a home address is given and service is made both on a
doubtful address for service, and the home address, the court can later deem service sufﬁcient.³
Endorsements on Documents
13.14(1) When the court clerk is presented with a commencement document for
ﬁling, the court clerk must
(a) endorse on the document an action number assigned to the action
by the court clerk, and the date that the document is ﬁled,
(b) ensure that the document to be ﬁled has endorsed on it the name of
the judicial centre where the document is ﬁled, and
(c) stamp the document as ﬁled.
(2) If a lawyer is acting for a person on whose behalf the action is started, when
the court clerk is presented with a commencement document for ﬁling, the court
clerk must ensure that the lawyer has endorsed on the document
(a) the name and address of the law ﬁrm,
(b) the name of the lawyer in the law ﬁrm in charge of the action, and
(c) the lawyer’s telephone number.
(3) When the court clerk is presented with a statement of defence or a demand
for notice for ﬁling, the court clerk must ensure that the document has endorsed
on it
(a) the name of the person ﬁling the document and, if the person ﬁling
the document is a lawyer, the same information as is required under
subrule (2) (a) to (c), and
(b) the defendant’s address for service.
(4) When the court clerk is presented with a document that is to be ﬁled in an
action after the action has started, the court clerk must
(a) endorse on the document the date that the document is ﬁled, and
(b) ensure that the document to be ﬁled has endorsed on it
(i) the name of the judicial centre at which the document is ﬁled,
and
(ii) the appropriate action number.
(5) If the court clerk is presented with a document that is to be ﬁled after an
action has started, the court clerk must also, if a lawyer is acting in respect of
the person on whose behalf the document is ﬁled, ensure that the lawyer has
endorsed on the document the same information as is required under subrule
(2) (a) to (c).

1 Goodzeck Est. v. Goodzeck 2019 NWTCA 4, Y #A1-AP-2019-000002 (one JA) (¶ 5).
2 Behm v. Hansen (#2) 2020 ABQB 52, JCE 4803 154552 (Jan 20) (¶’s 2-3) ; Rr.11.2(d), 11.15, 12.56;

13-71
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.14(6)
(6) When a document is ﬁled, the court clerk must note in the Court ﬁle, under
the action number assigned to the action by the court clerk, the fact that the
document was ﬁled.¹
A hidden trap lurks here which could have grave consequences. This R.13.14(1) (c) is
sometimes not followed by the clients. The Court of King’s Bench strongly discourages or
prevents lawyers trying to ﬁle documents at the counter in most circumstances. They have to be
ﬁled electronically. There are two ways of doing that. One is by the Filing Digital Service, and is
evidently superior. A list of what documents can be ﬁled there is found on https: //qg-ﬁling. alberta.
ca/. Otherwise, it sounds as though things can also be ﬁled by email, subject to detailed rules
about naming conventions and having an open account with the King’s Bench.
But the big danger with email ﬁling is that even if the document is accepted for ﬁling, it will
probably not be ﬁled the day it is received. It will be dated as received or “ﬁled” the day the Clerk’s
ofﬁce processes it and accepts it. Deadlines for ﬁling are becoming more important, and “ﬁling” is
now deﬁned (at least in these new announcements) as the date the Clerk gives to the document,
not the date of receipt. Under email ﬁling, the ﬁling date will not be backdated to the day of receipt.
So a deadline under the Limitations Act might be irretrievably lost by the Clerk’s delay. And a
deadline under the Rules might be also. If one is ﬁling something by email close to a deadline, and
the document is not eligible for ﬁling on the Filing Digital Service, there is a procedure for marking
the email and addressing it, to point out the urgency, but law ﬁrm letterhead is needed. What to
do if there is doubt or dispute about what kind of document is eligible for ﬁling on the Filing Digital
Service is not stated. Nor is it stated what happens if the document is rejected after the deadline
for some reason insufﬁcient to reject, or for something easily curable if notiﬁed earlier. (Filing
at the counter existed for some good reasons. ) At the least, law ﬁrms should be very careful to
record and print all steps in this process, with exact times and dates.²
An address for service is vital. Parties who do not give a proper address for service may well
lose any right to notice. With rare exceptions, a party is deemed to get notice when a document
reaches his address for service, even if he does not check his mail, is then travelling across the
Sahara, or has long since moved from that address.³

13.43 (court seal).

R.13.15 PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
When Document is Filed
13.15 A document is ﬁled when the court clerk of the judicial centre acknowledges
on the document that the document is ﬁled in the action.
Information Note
The appropriate judicial centre is determined under rule 3.3 [Determining the appropriate
judicial centre] and rule 3.4 [Claim for possession of land].

For grave dangers in electronic ﬁling of documents near a deadline, see R.13.14n.

Deviations From and Changes to Prescribed Forms
13.16 A prescribed form or a document prepared in place of a prescribed form is
not invalidated nor is there any contravention of these rules if there is a deviation
from or an addition to or omission from the form or document that
(a) does not adversely affect the substance of the information required
to be provided or that the Court requires to be provided, and
(b) is not intended to mislead.¹

Related Provisions
1.5 (non-compliance) ; 1.8 (Interpretation Act, s.26, does not apply) ; 3.2(4) (wrong form of

action); 12.4 (forms in family law) ; 13.6 (requirements of pleadings) ; 13.13 (requirements for
filed documents); 13.13(2) (prescribed forms to be used).
Deviations from form, even a lot of deviations, may be forgiven if no one was seriously harmed
or misled.²
It is otherwise if any question of form deprives a litigant of a substantive right.³
In context in the Customs Act, the word “form” does not encompass all documents provided
for by the Act, and is limited to means to collect and store information; it does not include a permit
or license.⁴
The Rules give different forms for statement of defences and for written applications, but
accidentally putting the wrong form number up in the corner of what is the correct heading and
wording of an application, causing no prejudice, was harmless and not a ground to dismiss the
application. Nor does the wrong form number in the corner affect how much advance notice of the
application is needed.⁵

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.17
Amendments to Records Other Than Commencement Documents,
Pleadings or Afﬁdavits
13.17 If the Court orders that an amendment be made to a document or other
record ﬁled with the Court, other than a commencement document, pleading, or
afﬁdavit,
(a) a note of the amendment must be attached to, made on, or made in
the document or record,
(b) the amendment must
(i) be dated and identiﬁed, and each amended version must be
identiﬁed, and
(ii) be endorsed by the court clerk in the following form:
Amended on [date] by [order]
Dated. ..
and
(c) except as required by this rule, the document or other record must
not be otherwise physically altered.¹

Related Provisions
3.62 and 3.63 (amending pleadings) ; 13.20 (changes to affidavits).

Subdivision 2
Form and Contents of Afﬁdavits and Exhibits
Types of Afﬁdavit
13.18(1) An afﬁdavit may be sworn
(a) on the basis of personal knowledge, or
(b) on the basis of information known to the person swearing the
afﬁdavit and that person’s belief.
(2) If an afﬁdavit is sworn on the basis of information and belief, the source of the
information must be disclosed in the afﬁdavit.²
(3) If an afﬁdavit is used in support of an application that may dispose of all
or part of a claim, the afﬁdavit must be sworn on the basis of the personal
knowledge of the person swearing the afﬁdavit.
Information Note
Under section 28(1) (ll) of the Interpretation Act, references to affidavits and to sworn
statements permit a person to make a solemn affirmation or solemn declaration instead of
an affidavit.

Footnote
1 Quite similar to previous 1968 R.140 mid. It came from 1914 R.269, via 1944 C. R.201. Cf.1897 Ont. R.315.
2 Quite similar to previous 1968 R.305(3) end. It was quite similar to 1914 R.416, and 1944 C. R.348, but the
requirement of the “source” was new in 1968. Rule 305(2) was virtually identical.

13-74
R.13.18(3) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES

Related Provisions
3.8(2) (affidavits used for originating applications) ; 6.7 (questioning on affidavits) ; 6.11

(evidence at applications) ; 8.17(2) (use of affidavits at trial).
A. Generally
Generally on afﬁdavits, see the discussion and authorities in the textbooks.¹
An afﬁdavit should only be taken for use as evidence in a suit then existing or contemplated. It
should not be sworn for other purposes, nor should it be done to tie a prospective witness down
to the story which he or she has told or agreed to. A proof of evidence (a “will say” statement) may
be signed, but should not be sworn.²
Counsel appearing in chambers and asserting facts to the court impliedly represents that all
those facts are sworn to (or afﬁrmed) in the ﬁled afﬁdavits or other ﬁled and admissible evidence,
on pain of heavy costs.³
Whether part or all of an afﬁdavit is admissible evidence or not, should be decided along
with the substantive application,⁴
rather than moving beforehand before a different chambers
judge to decide this as a preliminary point.⁵
For one thing, relevance or undue prejudice (beyond
probative value), the nature of the application pending and the intended use of the disputed
evidence should be considered.⁶
There does not now seem to be any equivalent in the present Rules to the 1968 Rules which
sometimes dictated who could be the deponent of an afﬁdavit, e. g. an ofﬁcer of a party.⁷
An afﬁdavit should not be struck out because it contains evidence which is irrelevant and so
inadmissible; that information should just be ignored.⁸
That is especially so when the matter is
begun by Originating Application, for then the pleadings are skimpy.⁹
But comments in an afﬁdavit
on the credibility of an opposing witness are relevant.¹⁰
See also the Can. Trustco case in Part B
below.
On form, see R.13.19 and notes. On deadlines for afﬁdavits, see Rr.3.11, 6.6, and 12.44.
In a civil case, the court may receive hearsay evidence if the opposing party consents or does
not object.¹¹

13-75
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.18(3)
B. Deponent’s Knowledge¹
1. Common-Law Exceptions to Hearsay Rule?
See also subpart B.10 below.
Rule 13.18(3) preserves not only a presumption of weakness of hearsay; it also gives a right
to meaningful cross-examination.²
Some common-law exceptions to the hearsay rule (such as admissions against interest)
remain, and they and such evidence are not barred by this Rule.³
The boundaries of that are
ill-explored, especially the modern “principled exceptions”. Though some cases⁴
suggest that
the Supreme Court of Canada’s modern common-law principled exceptions to the hearsay rule
overrule legislation such as R.13.18(3), L’Heureux-Dubé J. (in a concurring judgment) disagreed.⁵
The modern “principled exception” was used (in a child abduction/retention case) to admit and
give significant weight to, reports from Texas law enforcement and emergency services.⁶
Hearsay cannot be used in an afﬁdavit to get contempt of court found or punished.⁷
Nor to get
summary dismissal of even part of a claim.⁸
But where a large organization has to give an afﬁdavit about its records or its knowledge,
or about events over (say) 40 years ago, perfection is impossible, and the deﬁnition of what is
hearsay must be softened a little.⁹
On a summary judgment application, the need for that can be
softened when the applicant is a corporation or estate, where no one with personal knowledge
is left, or of course when one of the exceptions to the hearsay rule for trial evidence is present.¹⁰
If the Rules of Court make an ofﬁcer’s answers bind his employer, that does not itself make
them ﬁrst-hand evidence.¹¹

13-76
R.13.18(3) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
2. Unnecessary or Weak Hearsay
Even on an interlocutory application, the court is not required to accept hearsay on an
important topic, especially if the deponent does not swear that he believes its truth.¹
So third-hand
hearsay may be rejected.²
And so non-speciﬁc hearsay may be rejected.³
Second-hand or third-hand hearsay got very little weight, in one case.⁴
Some dangerous orders (such as Mareva injunctions) should not be entirely based on pure
hearsay, unless it is shown that ﬁrst-hand evidence is impossible.⁵
Courts are often critical of a party who does not swear her own afﬁdavit, and instead ﬁles
one by a clerk or legal assistant,⁶
or by his counsel.⁷
Or an administrator in a big corporation
thousands of miles from the detailed physical facts deposed to.⁸
But if the most likely person to
get the information or ﬁnd or keep it is a paralegal, there is no reason to criticize her being the
deponent.⁹
And an afﬁdavit merely reciting what counsel told the party has little weight.¹⁰
If one
exhibits correspondence merely to show that it was sent or received, the afﬁdavit can properly be
by the legal assistant of one of the counsel. That is not hearsay.¹¹

Footnote
1 ANC Timber v. Min. of Agric. , infra (¶’s 21, 58) ; Condo. Corp.0425177 v. Kuzio 2019 ABQB 814, JCE
1903 15696 (Oct 21) (¶’s 107-09). In constitutional litigation, certain paragraphs were struck from afﬁdavits
because they contained hearsay. The persons whose views were recounted to the afﬁants were known, so
one could not assume that they were not available to testify. It is unfair to use hearsay which cannot be cross-
examined on. Even an expert could be cross-examined on his evidence: Sahaluk v. Alta. Tpt. Safety Bd.
2013 ABQB 683, 576 AR 1; Lameman v. R.2012 ABCA 59, 522 AR 140 (Part I). A paragraph in an afﬁdavit
based entirely on other people’s evidence which has been struck out, should also be struck out: CCS Corp.
v. Secure Enr. Serv. (#2) 2013 ABQB 662 (¶’s 10-15), affd 2014 ABCA 390.
2 Alston v. Foothills (M. D. ) 2021 ABQB 951, JCC 1001 10269 (Nov 30) (¶’s 23-26) ; Lauder v. Owners
Condo. Plan 932 1565 (M) 2021 ABQB 145, JCE 1803 13413 (Feb 24) (¶ 24).
3 Lauder v. Condo. Plan 932 1565, supra (¶’s 22 ff. ).
4 L. Y. v. R. Y.2024 ABKB 209, JCC FL01 41775 (Apr 10) (¶’s 52-53).
5 Henenghaixin Corp. v. Deng 2022 ABCA 271, Calg 2101 0082 AC (Aug 22) (¶’s 30-31).
6 Shields, The Law of Afﬁdavits, Chapter 12 (LexisNexis). Jervis v. Nendze 2002 ABQB 673, 318 AR 293;
Parsons v. Chip Reit No.16 Ops.2006 ABQB 385; Calf Robe (M. C. R. ) v. A. -G. Can.2006 ABQB 652,
405 AR 366; Gerlitz v. Mawani 2010 ABQB 249, 506 AR 294 (¶’s 4-43) ; Re Pocklington 2017 ABQB
621, JCE B203 242454 (Oct 16) (¶’s 7-8, & n.2) (Semble exhibiting expert reports) ; Athwal v. Mather
(#1) 2019 ABQB 676, JCE 1703 03068 (Aug 30) (¶’s 10-11). Double hearsay in an afﬁdavit by a legal
assistant was not enough: Desoto Res. v. Encana Corp.2009 ABQB 512. An afﬁdavit by a legal assistant
is unacceptable except for noncontroversial matters, but it is done too often. Someone asserting prejudice
should himself swear, not ﬁle an afﬁdavit by his lawyer’s assistant, who is at two removes. Especially if the
prejudice asserted is unlikely in the context of the undisputed history of the suit: Tole v. Lucki 2017 ABCA
79, [2017] AJ #184 (one JA). But there is no invariable rule that a legal assistant can never give a sufﬁcient
afﬁdavit to support a motion. It depends on how much she knows and what is the topic: Spar Aerospace
v. Aerowerks Eng.2007 ABQB 543, 428 AR 84, affd 2008 ABCA 47, 85 Alta LR(4th) 19. An afﬁdavit of a
legal assistant as to the revival of the company and its amalgamation with another company sufﬁces where
corporate searches from the Alberta Corporate Registry system really prove what is in issue, and the legal
assistant is really just identifying them, not adding weight: Goska Nowak P. C. v. Robinson 2011 ABQB 385,
16 CPC(7th) 134. See also n. A above.
7 2007513 Alta. v. Pet Planet Franchise Corp.2022 ABCA 310, Calg 2201 0122 AC (Sep 29) (¶’s 18, 21).
See also Part C below, and Shields, op. cit. supra, Chapter 11.
8 C. I. B. C. v. Strihavka (M) 2019 ABQB 835, JCStP 1914 00218 (Oct 30).
9 Walker v. Walker 2013 ABCA 213, 553 AR 166. And see n. A above.
10 S. N. v. R.2006 ABQB 115, 396 AR 7.
11 Alston v. Foothills (M. D. ) 2021 ABQB 951, JCC 1001 10269 (Nov 30) (¶ 22).

13-77
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.18(3)
However, an afﬁdavit not tendered for ﬁnal relief or ending the suit can contain hearsay, by R.
13.18(1) ’s express terms. A chambers judge cannot nullify that Rule by saying such hearsay gets
no weight.¹
It is especially bad to give it no weight when it is corroborated and not cross-examined
on, and the opponent ﬁles no evidence at all, despite many chances to do so.²
Someone not the author of some opinion cannot put it into evidence by merely exhibiting it to
an afﬁdavit and calling it a “report”; opinion evidence is generally inadmissible.³
Nor will calling it
a “survey” do, if it is too tendentiously gathered.⁴
That is also true of academic articles.⁵
And even
more true of a news story or an internet item.⁶
Nor can one exhibit to one’s afﬁdavit, interrogatory
answers, especially if they are not by the deponent but are from the party ﬁling the afﬁdavit, as
one cannot rely on one’s own discovery answers.⁷
An afﬁdavit was struck out, as the relevant part
of its contents was a document tendered on the contents of the document, but nothing was said
in the afﬁdavit to show the truth of those contents.⁸
Like all evidence, an afﬁdavit may be excluded if its prejudicial effect outweighs its testimonial
value (deﬁning prejudice broadly).⁹
Live evidence and hearsay are not the only possible types of hearsay admissible on an
application, even one for ﬁnal relief.¹⁰
Giving one’s own interpretation of statements made in another language, without swearing to
belief, is inadmissible hearsay. The afﬁant’s own translation of what was said is useless if what
was translated was an unofﬁcial transcription from a tape, with no one swearing to the accuracy
of the transcription, or even to belief in that. Nor are some extracts (sworn to) of much weight, as
the parts not quoted may have qualiﬁed the ones quoted.¹¹
3. Negative Evidence
Swearing to a negative is difﬁcult. For example, that a business got no notice of something.
Some degree of hearsay is needed when someone wants to show that information has not been
provided, especially provided to a group or organization bigger than 6 or so people. Sometimes
direct evidence is not possible.¹²
4. Documentary Evidence
On documents in another language, see subpart 2 above.

R.13.18(3) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
On an application for ﬁnal relief, the afﬁant must have a general knowledge of documents
attached to an afﬁdavit.¹
Indeed, where documentary evidence is involved, the exclusionary rule is absolute. The
common law provides that (subject to some exceptions), the court cannot receive secondary
evidence of a paper or other record. It must have primary evidence, i. e. , the original paper. A
copy or a recital will not do.²
A paraphrase in an afﬁdavit of someone else’s report was therefore
rejected as not the best evidence.³
Certiﬁed copies of ofﬁcial documents do not violate R.13.18.
The exception to the rule against secondary evidence permits its admission.⁴
Excerpts from a transcript of a preliminary inquiry (in a different proceeding) and uncertiﬁed
copies of court documents (ditto) are hearsay. Use of someone else’s notes of a hearing is
hearsay, unless at a time when one truly recalled the details, one had checked them for accuracy.⁵
Swearing to a bookkeeping system and to the entries in it may not be hearsay.⁶
And an ofﬁcer
of an organization with access to all records can review them and give personal evidence of what
occurred many years before.⁷
That need not be the best-informed person,⁸
and if the Canada
Evidence Act applies, that is not conﬁned to bookkeeping records.⁹
One can identify a document
in an afﬁdavit without having drafted it or even read it in full.¹⁰
Hearsay may be proper for questions
of general knowledge, such as the general knowledge which creditors would have of a certain
fact.¹¹
Some documents generated in a business are pure inadmissible hearsay, e. g. ones which do
not record business data and are not kept for that, and give no context.¹²
A document possessed by a company and known by a responsible ofﬁcer (or employee in
the course of his duties) is evidence that the company had knowledge of the contents or the

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.18(3)
connection with the document. It may even be evidence of its contents, if the company recognized,
adopted, or acted on the contents without showing necessity and reliability.¹
5. Opposing an Application
Rule 13.18(3) only bars hearsay supporting a ﬁnal application, so hearsay can be used in
an afﬁdavit opposing such an application,²
such as opposing summary judgment.³
A respondent
resisting an application may have to use hearsay if he has no personal knowledge of the facts.
The judge can decide what weight to give the hearsay.⁴
So if a party seeks a summary disposition
of the suit or part of it, that party cannot rely on hearsay (information and belief), even if it is the
respondent.⁵
6. Non-Final Applications
A motion to amend pleadings is interlocutory, and so hearsay in an afﬁdavit to get such leave
is permissible. The order does not ﬁnally dispose of the rights of the parties.⁶
Case management
makes no ﬁnal disposition, and hearsay is admissible.⁷
Judicial review is a ﬁnal disposition, so
hearsay is not admissible.⁸
The party moving to set aside service is not necessarily moving to end the suit, especially
if the time to serve has not yet expired. And the plaintiff who wants a trial is not seeking a ﬁnal
order. Only an afﬁdavit supporting a motion to end the suit has to be ﬁrst-hand knowledge, under
R.13.18(2).⁹
7. Source of Hearsay¹⁰
Even when one can put in hearsay evidence by afﬁdavit, it is important to comply with R.
13.18(2) which says that the source of the information is necessary.¹¹
A company will not do as
a source. Omission of such information where required makes the court disregard the offending
part.¹²
If the afﬁdavit does not say that the deponent believes each of the pieces of information,

R.13.18(3) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
in general the court must disregard the ones not expressly believed.¹
But the necessary belief
in hearsay may be implied, and need not be express in every paragraph of the afﬁdavit.²
The
word “verily” adds nothing, except to suggest that whoever drafted the afﬁdavit believes in magic
incantations. In practice, the grounds for belief can rarely be given unless the deponent recites the
precise source, e. g. : “I am informed by Jane Smith that. . . . ”
Rule 13.18 requires source of the information, but that does not apply to information elicited
by cross-examination on an afﬁdavit. No Rule extends that doctrine to cross-examination, and
the witness cross-examined has no duty to insist that the counsel cross-examining follow up with
more questions.³
Subrule (3) only requires that the information in the afﬁdavit be known personally by the afﬁant;
it does not require that he or she say that the knowledge is personal, nor use words like “swear
positively”.⁴
Swearing to a translation of a transcript has little value as evidence, if the transcript is
incomplete and no one swears to the accuracy of the transcription.⁵
8. Admissions
One can get summary judgment without infringing the hearsay rules, if one relies on an
admission in pleadings of the respondent, or a formal admission by the respondent in open
court.⁶
Rule 13.18 does not apply to cross-examination on afﬁdavit in the same suit, all of whose
transcript is before the court.⁷
9. Effect of Rule’s Breach
On whether to strike out an afﬁdavit before the application for which it was ﬁled is heard, see
Part A above. On consent or non-objection to inadmissible evidence, see Part A above.
If an afﬁdavit contains hearsay contrary to R.13.18, the court should ignore the offending
passages (not receive them as evidence), but should weigh the other non-hearsay passages.⁸
Having a separate motion in advance to strike the offending afﬁdavit is a dubious proposition,
though clear notice of the objection is needed.⁹
An afﬁdavit worded as though it were ﬁrst-hand
knowledge, with no source for most of its statements, many of them (e. g. how a ﬁre occurred)
which could not have been ﬁrst-hand knowledge, was largely struck out.¹⁰
See also the Sucker
Creek case at the end of Part A above.

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.18(3)
If there is an application to a single justice of appeal to strike out some evidence ﬁled for a
pending appeal, whether that one justice of appeal can decide that is not clear.¹
10. Other Types of Evidence
Though this Rule often bars hearsay in an afﬁdavit, no Rule always requires an afﬁdavit, and
R.7.3 allows “other evidence” to support a summary judgment motion, so a party can rely on a
(ﬁrst-hand) afﬁdavit by someone else.²
Sometimes someone with expertise can testify even without formally going through the
procedure to qualify her as an expert witness. Someone involved personally in the underlying
events because of her expertise, can be allowed to explain why she acted as she did, even if she
strays into her expertise.³
A lesser-known exception to the rule against opinion evidence, allows what may be on the
fuzzy border between fact and opinion, where a seeming opinion is given as a shorthand way of
expressing a number of small facts lying behind a conclusion, e. g. that Smith was drunk. The lay
witness has to be in a better position than the trial judge to make the conclusion, it is one ordinary
non-experts can form, this witness has experience enough, and the underlying facts are too
subtle or complicated to narrate effectively without stating a conclusion.⁴
C. Substance of Afﬁdavit
On drawing inferences from afﬁdavit evidence, see Rr.7.3 n. B, and 8.15. On inferences in
afﬁdavits, see Kissel case.⁵
A great many afﬁdavits are carelessly drawn. Often they contain minor factual errors which
detract from their credibility, swear positively to what is only hearsay, yet omit facts known to the
witness which are necessary or would be useful for the application.
Even ﬁrst-hand information in an afﬁdavit not cross-examined on need not get much weight if
it is bald conclusions or otherwise of poor quality.⁶
A lawyer now acting for one of the parties should not be an afﬁant (give an afﬁdavit) in the suit,
if he or she can help it.⁷
There are 3 reasons for this prohibition:
1. the lawyer’s ﬁrm then may well be disqualiﬁed from appearing on the relevant application;
2. often the lawyer lacks personal knowledge;⁸
3. the lawyer is then exposed to cross-examination on anything relevant to the application
pending,⁹
which will lead to ugly arguments over waiver of privilege.¹⁰

R.13.18(3) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
But a lawyer no longer acting may swear an afﬁdavit for use against a former client, if no valid
claim of privilege is made, and no other rule of law excludes that evidence.¹
When the afﬁant is not a lawyer, the afﬁdavit should be written in plain words which the afﬁant
(deponent) can understand clearly. There should be no legalese, difﬁcult words, nor long complex
sentences. Otherwise the witness may not understand. And if he is caught in a lie, he may later
be tempted to claim that his lawyer misled him. An afﬁdavit which is plainly in the language of
the lawyer and not the afﬁant, lacks credibility. An afﬁdavit should be full and frank. Boilerplate
language at best bulks out the afﬁdavit uselessly, and at worst could trick the reader into thinking
that it means what it does not. There should be no strategic omissions.²
An afﬁdavit should give relevant facts, not a “presentation of [that party’s] argument”.³
On
the other hand, part of the narrative can also be given, to help understand relevance and the
relationship of various facts.⁴
There is much law on this.⁵
An afﬁdavit which merely deposes to
what one party will argue swears to nothing, and is useless as evidence.⁶
Nor should an afﬁdavit
contain argument or conclusions.⁷
Bald conclusory allegations or speculation or opinion, lacking speciﬁc facts, often get very
little weight.⁸
It is hard to prove a negative, and one usually cannot expect much in the way of details of a
denial.⁹
An afﬁdavit should not swear to facts in the alternative,¹⁰
swear to law¹¹
nor how to interpret a
written contract, nor contain opinions which the deponent does not show that he is qualiﬁed to

give.¹
An afﬁdavit was struck out because it was not fact-related but was a lawyer’s opinion on
a legal question.²
On afﬁdavits by experts giving their opinions, see Rr.5.39, 6.11, and 7.3 n. B.
An afﬁdavit, even by a professor, on legal questions or points of policy known to the court, will

Expert evidence in an afﬁdavit for a motion must follow the same basic requirements as trial

backed up by a letter of opinion by a possible expert, which letter is not sworn.⁵
An afﬁdavit should not contain attempts to relitigate matters already decided (even the basis
for the order disobeyed which led to the present contempt proceedings), nor irrelevant matters.⁶
The Law Society and the courts forbid lawyers to “notarize” strange pseudolegal documents
not based on any real law, and have severely criticized lawyers who persist in this. One such
lawyer had to pay the Clerk $10, 000 under R.10.49, and the Law Society was notiﬁed.⁷
