Requirements for Afﬁdavits
13.19(1) In addition to complying with rule 13.13 [Requirements for all filed
documents], an afﬁdavit under these rules must comply with all of the following:
(a) be in Form 49,
(b) state, on the front page, the full name of the person swearing the
afﬁdavit and the date the afﬁdavit was sworn,¹
(c) state the place of residence of the person swearing the afﬁdavit,
(d) be written in the ﬁrst person,²
(e) be divided into consecutively numbered paragraphs, with dates and
numbers expressed in numerals unless words or a combination of
words and numerals makes the meaning clearer,³
(f) be signed or acknowledged and sworn before a person empowered
to administer oaths, whether that person prepared the afﬁdavit or
not,⁴
(g) contain a statement of when, where and before whom the afﬁdavit
was sworn, and
(h) be signed by the person administering the oath.
(2) An afﬁdavit is not invalid or otherwise improper just because it was sworn
before a commencement document was ﬁled.⁵
[Alta. Reg.124/10;163/10]
Information Note
The place of residence referred to in subrule (1) (c) could be an address or the municipality
in which the person swearing the affidavit resides.

R.13.19(2) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES

Related Provisions
6.11(2) (use of unfiled affidavits; 13.13 (requirements for filed documents) ; 13.21

(requirements for exhibits to affidavits).
Generally on oaths, see the Ferguson book.¹
On the form and technical requirements for an
afﬁdavit, see Shields.²
Rule 13.19(f) reverses the old Rule³
that the commissioner could not be the solicitor for one
of the parties.
However, it is very wise for the commissioner to be meticulous in administering the oath with
a Bible,⁴
if only for self-protection. A witness later caught in perjury in an afﬁdavit will often claim
that he did not know that he was taking an oath, and that he merely signed some papers which
his lawyer gave him, without understanding them. The commissioner will then be accused of the
offence of having certiﬁed something to be sworn which was not really sworn. (So it might be safer
to use an independent commissioner. )⁵
A court will not strike out an afﬁdavit for want of an oath if there was any attempt to invoke
the Deity,⁶
or if the oath bind’s the afﬁant’s conscience.⁷
The Evidence Acts allow alternates to an
oath.⁸
Curiously enough, some courts have been very picky about omissions from the jurat (the
paragraph just above the commissioner’s signature).⁹
A notice about commissioning afﬁdavits issued during the Covid pandemic gave new
requirements, which an afﬁdavit of records did not meet. That could be defective commissioning,
but that does not upset the disclosure function of the Afﬁdavit under R.4.33. And s.49 of the
Alberta Evidence Act says that informalities in an afﬁdavit taken before a commissioner for oaths
do not bar a court from receiving it in evidence if the court sees proper.¹⁰
There is a sharp distinction between administering an oath, and signing the jurat to certify that
that has been done. The oath is complete without the jurat, and the jurat is merely a means of
easy proof that the oath was administered.¹¹
It is improper for a lawyer to act as a notary public endorsing or “notarizing” odd pseudo-legal
documents with no legal basis, to help a self-represented litigant, especially if these documents
threaten court staff and try to override existing court documents and the new document purports
to be some kind of legal order or judgment. The Law Society has repeatedly cautioned lawyers
not to do that.¹²

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.20
Changes in Afﬁdavits
13.20 An afﬁdavit with an insertion, alteration or erasure must not be used
without the Court’s permission unless the insertion, alteration or erasure is
authenticated by the initials of the person administering the oath.¹

Requirements for Exhibits to Afﬁdavit
13.21(1) A record to be used with an afﬁdavit must be
(a) an exhibit to the afﬁdavit, and
(b) identiﬁed by a certiﬁcate of the person administering the oath.²
(2) If the total number of pages of an afﬁdavit and attached exhibits is 25 or more,
(a) the exhibits must be separated by tabs, and the pages within each
tab must be numbered consecutively, or
(b) the pages of the afﬁdavit and all exhibits must be consecutively
numbered using a single series of numbers.³
(3) An exhibit to an afﬁdavit must be attached or appended to the afﬁdavit when
the afﬁdavit is ﬁled unless the exhibit is adequately identiﬁed and the afﬁdavit
states a reason for not attaching or appending the exhibit, which includes the
following:
(a) the physical characteristics of the exhibit,
(b) the conﬁdentiality of the exhibit or its content;
(c) the exhibit has already been ﬁled;
(d) any other appropriate reason.
(4) Exhibits that are not attached to an afﬁdavit under subrule (3) shall be
available for inspection and copying by the other parties to the litigation, subject
to the direction of the Court.
(5) Any interested person who is not a party to the action may apply to the Court
for access to the exhibit, which may be granted on such terms and conditions as
the Court may direct. [Alta. Reg.124/10;194/20]

13-88
R.13.22(1) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
On exhibits to afﬁdavits as hearsay, see R.13.18 n. B. Generally on exhibits, see Shields.¹
A vague reference in an afﬁdavit to a videodisc containing many diverse documents did not
sufﬁce. A marked exhibit was lacking.²
Rules about broadcasting recordings of proceedings were interpreted as conﬁned to sounds
heard during the court hearing. They do not include a video recording of some previous event,
which recording was merely put into evidence at the hearing. Hearings and exhibits are distinct.
An exhibit is not part of the proceedings, and is almost always created before the proceedings.
Access to exhibits is a corollary of the open-court principle. The Charter gives presumptive rights
to broadcast electronic exhibits, but the court must weigh that against impacts on fairness of trial.³
Afﬁdavits as exhibits to other afﬁdavits were criticized.⁴
The 2020 amendment to this Rule broadens the grounds for the court to let a party not attach
an exhibit to the ﬁled original afﬁdavit, and allows for access to and inspection of that exhibit in
that event.
Where an afﬁdavit exhibited only the ﬁrst and last (signed) pages of an assignment, that was
held proper, because all that was sworn to was the fact of the assignment, not its terms.⁵
Afﬁdavits by Visually Impaired or Those Unable to Read
13.22(1) If it seems to the person administering the oath that the person swearing
the afﬁdavit is visually impaired or unable to read, the person administering the
oath must read the afﬁdavit to the person swearing the afﬁdavit and certify that
(a) the afﬁdavit was read to the person,
(b) the person seemed to understand it, and
(c) the person signed the afﬁdavit or made the person’s mark in the
presence of the person administering the oath.
(2) The afﬁdavit must not be used in evidence without the certiﬁcation referred
to in subrule (1) unless the Court is satisﬁed that the afﬁdavit was read to, and
appeared to be understood by, the person swearing it.⁶

Where the deponent is blind or does not readily understand English, the proper procedure
must be observed with particular care.⁷

1 Shields, The Law of Afﬁdavits, Chapter 17 (LexisNexis).

3 C. B. C. (Soc. Radio-Can. ) v. Dufour & The Queen 2011 SCC 3, [2011] 1 SCR 65.
4 Carbone v. Whidden 2015 ABCA 161, Calg 1401 0135 AC (one JA May 8).
5 CWB Maxium Finl. v.2026998 Alta.2020 ABQB 733, JCE 2003 04457 (Nov 25). But the rule against
secondary evidence of a document was not mentioned.

13-89
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.23(1)
Understanding Afﬁdavit
13.23(1) If it seems to the person administering the oath that the person swearing
a written afﬁdavit does not understand the language in which the afﬁdavit is
written, before the afﬁdavit is sworn the contents of the afﬁdavit must be
translated for the person swearing the afﬁdavit by a person competent to do so.
(2) Before the afﬁdavit is translated, the translator must be sworn to accurately
translate the afﬁdavit and oath.
(3) The person administering the oath must certify as to the person’s belief that
the afﬁdavit was translated for the person swearing the afﬁdavit by the sworn
translator.¹
(4) Unless otherwise permitted by the Court, a sworn afﬁdavit that is not in the
English language must be translated into English by a translator competent to
do so and, when the afﬁdavit is ﬁled, be accompanied with a certiﬁcate of the
translator that the translation is accurate and complete.

Related Provisions
6.19 (interpreters during questioning)

Generally on the language of afﬁdavits, see Shields.²
An English statute of 1731 requiring the use of the English language in courts was held
applicable and in force in British Columbia, so therefore a document prepared in another language
(French) before suit brought, had to be translated into English if it was to be exhibited to an
afﬁdavit.³
The Supreme Court of Canada reafﬁrms and restates a party’s (or even an intervener’s)
or a witness’s, right to use either ofﬁcial language, entirely at that person’s (witness’s) choice,
irrespective of why, or how well he or she speaks the other language, and irrespective of costs or
delay or practicalities.⁴
Bilingual legislation in Alberta is optional, not mandatory, and so English-only Acts and
Regulations are valid.⁵
More Than One Individual Swearing Afﬁdavit
13.24 An afﬁdavit may be made by 2 or more individuals and that fact must be
stated in the statement of when, where and before whom the afﬁdavit was sworn.

R.13.25 PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
Use of Filed Afﬁdavits
13.25 In an application or proceeding in an action, a party may use and refer to
any afﬁdavit ﬁled in the action.¹

Related Provisions
6.7 (questioning on affidavits) ; 6.11 (evidence at applications) ; 8.17(2) (use of affidavits at

trial); 8.19 (later use of trial evidence).
Rule 13.25 allows use of an earlier afﬁdavit, as Family Law Practice Note No.2 limits parties
to one afﬁdavit for any particular application, barring court leave.²
When a key witness unexpectedly died, after he ﬁled an afﬁdavit but before he was cross-
examined on the afﬁdavit, the afﬁdavit and his examination for discovery answers were allowed
as trial evidence, using the common-law tests for hearsay evidence of necessity and reliability,
necessity not having to be absolute.³
Prejudice merely from having to face some relevant or
important evidence is not a bar to such paper evidence at trial, nor are small contradictions
between the two pieces of paper evidence.⁴
This is not about an afﬁdavit ﬁled in another lawsuit.⁵
Exhibits: Filing and Return
13.26(1) Exhibits ﬁled with the court clerk in a hearing or trial must
(a) be dated and numbered,
(b) indicate the parties involved in the action, application or proceeding
for which the exhibit is ﬁled, and
(c) state who owns the exhibit and by whom the exhibit is ﬁled.
(2) A list of exhibits ﬁled in the hearing or trial, brieﬂy describing the exhibits and
who ﬁled them, must be noted in the Court ﬁle.
(3) After the time for an appeal has expired, the court clerk may, subject to any
order, return a record or exhibit to the owner or person who ﬁled it.
(4) The court clerk may destroy or dispose of a record or exhibit if the court clerk
(a) gives at least 3 months’ written notice by ordinary mail to the owner
or person who ﬁled it of the court clerk’s intention to destroy or
dispose of the record or exhibit, and
(b) receives no response to the notice by the end of the notice period
given under clause (a).⁶
[Alta. Reg.124/10;163/10]

13-91
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.26(4)

Related Provisions
13.44 (duties of clerk)

A. References
On exhibits generally, see the textbooks,¹
and R.13.21 and its note. On rejecting an exhibit as
hearsay, see R.13.18 n. B.
Hyperlinks to webpages are not admissible evidence because they may not be static. A
YouTube video or any other webpage online today could be altered or gone tomorrow. What an
afﬁant swears to has to be knowable, and not altered since he or she swore. So must the record,
for anyone looking later, including the Court of Appeal.²
B. Nature of Exhibits
It is doubtful that there is any particular magic in marking a document or other object as
an exhibit. The question is one of substance and not form. If the object or document has been
tendered in evidence and accepted as evidence, it does not matter that everyone forgot to mark it
as an exhibit, or that it had earlier only been marked for identiﬁcation.³
Making a document an exhibit at trial makes it evidence, which can both help and hurt the
party doing so.⁴
Case law on documents in another language is reviewed and whether translation is needed.⁵
C. Custody and Inspection
An appeal was restored to the list, where much of the delay was caused by the government’s
Exhibit Central giving away the original exhibits from trial, before time to appeal had expired, in
breach of R.13.26.⁶
The common-law rule is that all papers ﬁled in the Clerk’s ofﬁce are open to inspection by all
members of the public. That includes court pleadings, written arguments such as factums, and
ﬁled evidence, whether it be appeal books or exhibits which have been ﬁled at a trial.⁷
There only
seem to be ﬁve real exceptions to this public right:
1. Reasonableness and convenience of hours or access to documents, when court
resources are limited or when court staff themselves need access to the documents.
2. No right of access to documents which have been marked for identiﬁcation only, but then
ruled inadmissible and not admitted as exhibits.⁸

ff. (2d ed.2010) ; Shields, The Law of Afﬁdavits, Chapter 17 (LexisNexis).
2 C. M. v. R. (Alta. ) 2022 ABKB 716, JCE 2203 04046 (Oct 26) (¶ 44).
3 L. S. v. Dir. of Child, Youth & Fam. Enhancement 2009 ABCA 10, 446 AR 135. See the C. P. E. , Chapter 47,
Part D.

R.13.26(4) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
3. Limited power to keep certain evidence or papers conﬁdential where necessary, i. e. there
will be grave harm without such a sealing order,¹
and all competing interests have been
weighed, and there is no narrower restriction which will sufﬁce.²
One may compare the
general principles when the court may keep oral proceedings private.³
4. Some express legislation requiring that court ﬁles be sealed, e. g. in adoption matters.⁴
5. Publication bans and sealing court ﬁles are not the same as a right to copy tape
recordings or other real exhibits.⁵
The Court may give a right to record court proceedings
for accuracy testing, not for broadcasting. Public right to access is to information, not
format. There is no right to copy such tapes or evidence.⁶
Where most of a diary was irrelevant but the book was marked as an exhibit, the court let it be
withdrawn and replaced with photostats of the relevant entries.⁷
Alberta’s Freedom of Information
and Protection of Privacy Act does not apply to information on a court ﬁle, which is not limited to
a cardboard folder and the papers in it; also exempt is a list of basic information culled from such
ﬁles and grouped by date and place of hearing.⁸
In a suit against disclosure of conﬁdences, the
court ﬁle was properly sealed (in both courts) to prevent serious risk to an important commercial
interest, where beneﬁts outweighed harm.⁹
A party was allowed to borrow an exhibit from the court record so that an expert could use it
to produce computer versions, but on condition that the other party have somebody present to
watch the process.¹⁰

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.27
An Anton Piller was obtained and the ﬁle at ﬁrst sealed, but later on the party seeking the
order abandoned all the proceedings. The court thought that sealing the ﬁle was not necessary,
but said that a few documents were not to be available to the public and edited a few paragraphs
out of a few more.¹
A tribunal has “deliberative” secrecy for the content of and substance of its decision making
and deliberations, but not for the formal process which it established to ensure consistency.²
D. Withdrawing Evidence³
Though the topic is not mentioned in the Rules, common law sets up guidelines for when
a party can withdraw a ﬁled afﬁdavit, e. g. whether there is a motion pending, reason for the
ﬁling, reason for withdrawal, and whether there would be prejudice to another party or to the
administration of justice. Here the parties compromised a matrimonial dispute, the application
had not been heard, and the nasty afﬁdavits had not been used, and both parties wanted them
removed, so removal was allowed.⁴
Subdivision 3
Lost and Concurrent Documents, Certiﬁed Copies,
Authenticated Photographs and Video Recordings
Lost Documents
13.27 If a commencement or other document has been lost, the court clerk, if
satisﬁed of the loss and of the correctness of a copy of the document, may
certify the copy, which may be used in place of the original.⁵

At one time the court did not keep the original commencement document, making loss more
likely, but now the court keeps it. Conversely, some Court of King’s Bench ﬁles are disorganized
and incomplete.
Concurrent Document
13.28(1) During the validity of a commencement document, the court clerk may
issue a concurrent document, which is in force during the validity of the original
document.
(2) The concurrent document must have the same date as the original document
and must be marked “concurrent” with the date of issue of the concurrent
document.⁶

Related Provisions

On exhibits generally, see the textbooks,²
and R.13.26 n.
Apart from legislation about use of certiﬁed copies, at common law court records are
admissible in evidence, without notice.³
On a motion to strike pleadings, uncertiﬁed copies of
court documents, and ofﬁcial reports in the professional discipline proceedings in question, none
attached to any afﬁdavit, were received, as they were the record on which the plaintiff founded his
claim, and he did not object to their presentation.⁴
Court pleadings are inadmissible in another suit to prove the facts in them, but do prove that
such suits were ﬁled.⁵
The pleadings and a contract settling another earlier suit are admissible
only to show the fact that those proceedings existed, and what was in issue in them.⁶
A discussion of how to prove a previous conviction, using the common-law rules, is found in
the Albright case.⁷

The court cannot order a non-party to produce records to assist discovery processes (here

Maybe a judge or court should warn parties before the judge looks at a document on the court

and why, but notice of that is not needed if the knowledge already exists from somewhere else.²

Authenticated Photographs of Personal Property
13.30(1) If a party wishes to put personal property in evidence, the party may, in
addition to or in substitution for the property, ﬁle one or more photographs
(a) if permitted or directed by the Court, or
(b) with the agreement of every other party.
(2) The party ﬁling the photographs must provide an accompanying certiﬁcate by
the photographer certifying its authenticity and, with respect to each photograph,
(a) the date, time and place where the photograph was taken;
(b) the photographer’s name;
(c) whether the photograph has been modiﬁed, enhanced or altered in
any way, or otherwise tampered with;
(d) any other matter directed by the Court.
(3) An authenticated photograph ﬁled under this rule must be treated by the
Court in the same manner as if the personal property had been received in
evidence, but if in the Court’s opinion the quality or content of a photograph is
not adequate, the Court may
(a) direct that the photograph not be used, or
(b) give any other direction the Court considers appropriate in the
circumstances.⁴

This Rule is important, for it removes the biggest objection to commission evidence, that
the trial judge will not see or hear the witness. Rules 6.10 and 8.18 also allow live evidence by
telephone or television.
Parties are not to make private sound recordings of court proceedings.²
DIVISION 5
PAYMENT OF FEES AND ALLOWANCES,
AND WAIVERS OF FEES
Fees and Allowances
13.32(1) In every action, application or proceeding in Court, there must be paid
to the appropriate court ofﬁcer or other appropriate person the fee speciﬁed,
referred to or determined in accordance with Schedule B [Court Fees and
Witness and Other Allowances] unless the court clerk waives the fee, in whole
or in part, in accordance with guidelines, if any, established or adopted by the
Minister of Justice for persons unable to pay fees.
(2) Except for fees for transcripts, the fees referred to in subrule (1) are payable
in advance unless the court clerk otherwise permits.
[Alta. Reg.1241/10;143/11;170/12;216/22]

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.33(1)

Related Provisions
5.40 (experts attendance at trial) ; 6.17 (payment of allowance) ; 6.38 (requiring attendance for

A court hearing fee (on top of a commencement fee) was held unconstitutional as hindering
access to justice, as it did not contain appropriate exemptions (not restricted to for indigent
litigants), nor provide the court with a discretion to waive the fee in appropriate cases.¹
The complete Alberta Ministerial Order 22/2020 providing guidelines to permit a court clerk
or registrar to waive certain fees payable to court ofﬁcials, can be found in Volume 2 of this
Handbook under Schedule B: Court Fees and Witness and Other Allowances.
A habeas corpus applicant was denied a fee waiver because the court lacked jurisdiction
over the matter, and no loss of residual liberty, nor detention, was shown, only failure to release
early.²
Fee waivers are based on the plaintiff’s stated income, and over 41% of recent statements of
claim struck out as vexatious had a fee waiver. Such plaintiffs are often judgment proof and some
bring many lawsuits. A plaintiff who ﬁled 14 such suits lost his or her waivers and would need
special judicial permission for future waivers.³
Where a non-lawyer ﬁled for someone else an abusive habeas corpus application, retroactively
the court revoked the waiver of ﬁling fees previously granted.⁴
Now the subordinate legislation
bars fee waivers for those declared vexatious litigants, and if they got leave for more litigation, it
would have to be on condition that they pay the usual ﬁling fees.⁵
Uncertainty of Amount of Fees and Allowances
13.33(1) If the amount of a fee, allowance or other amount is uncertain or
impossible to determine, the fee or amount may be estimated by the court clerk
and adjusted when the fee or amount is ﬁxed by a judge.⁶
(2) When a person is paid or given an allowance before actual attendance at an
application or proceeding conducted under these rules, the person is entitled to
receive any additional sum that is determined to be payable after completion of
the attendance.
(3) When a party is permitted or required to pay an allowance, that party may
have the amount ﬁxed by an assessment ofﬁcer without notice to any other
person, subject to adjustment after completion of the actual attendance.

13-98
R.13.34 PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES

Related Provisions
6.17 (allowance for witness) ; 10.31 (costs award) ; Schedule B (allowances)

These advance payments used to be called “conduct money”.
A witness’s allowance is only an estimate of the fees and disbursements which a witness will
be entitled to. In most cases that estimate must be paid to the witness in cash before the witness
is obliged to attend. Usually it is calculated from the witness’ home to the place of testifying and
back, including the cost of transportation, hotel, and meals. But sometimes a party unnecessarily
selecting a witness far away, or a party who has moved far away during litigation, may get a reduced
allowance.¹
For cross-examination on afﬁdavits ﬁled for summary judgment, an allowance may
have to be provided by the party moving for summary judgment, whether he ﬁled the afﬁdavit or
is cross-examining on it, unless there are special circumstances or near certainty of the motion’s
succeeding.²
Rule 5.19 was somehow applied to excuse a plaintiff for having to pay a witness
allowance when she sued somebody in another province!³
Rule 13.33 allows a party to have the court ﬁx the estimate, so that the witness cannot refuse
to attend by alleging insufﬁcient tender.⁴
But once the case is over, there has to be an accounting: the witness will have to refund any
excess (says judge-made law), and will be entitled to be paid any shortfall (says R.13.33(2) ).⁵
Costs taxable by a winning party against a losing party at the end cover everything properly
payable to the witnesses, and so include full witness fees and expenses, not merely what (if
anything) was tendered as conduct money.
Fee Accounts
13.34 The court clerk may establish and operate accounts for the purpose of
allowing lawyers to charge fees or other amounts to the account and for the
court clerk to send an invoice for fees and other amounts as required.⁶

Fee Exemption
13.35(1) A peace ofﬁcer is exempt from the payment of fees or other amounts
under Schedule B [Court Fees and Witness and Other Allowances] for the search
of a name, the inspection of a ﬁle or a copy or the certiﬁcation of a document
when the Court service is required in the execution or discharge of the peace
ofﬁcer’s duties.

litigant and suggested that the automatic exemption for the fee on ﬁling an appeal be replaced
with need for waiver by a Justice of Appeal, on evidence of some prospect of success.³

DIVISION 6
JUDGE’S FIAT, COURT OFFICERS AND
COURT REPORTERS
Judge’s Fiat
13.38(1) A judge or an applications judge may authorize, direct or give permission
to a court ofﬁcer to do an act, and a note signed by the judge or applications
judge is sufﬁcient authority to carry out the act.²
(2) The authorization, direction or permission must be ﬁled unless the judge or
applications judge otherwise directs.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;136/22]

Related Provisions
1.4(2) (f) (power to give approvals) ; 1.4(3) (directions by judge) ; 9.1 (form of orders) ; 9.15

(variation of orders)
A ﬁat is a brief written direction or permission by a judge. Aside from its form, it is an order. It is
often handwritten or rubber stamped on the margin or cover of another document. It may read like
this: “Let this document be ﬁled, despite noncompliance with Rule 9.99. A. B. Smith, A. J. ”
If given without notice and without jurisdiction, a ﬁat may be set aside.³
A ﬁat is an order as much as any other, and on whatever part of a document it is written, even
a Land Titles document. Refusal to obey is contempt. If it is thought wrong, someone has to apply
to set it aside.⁴

r.468, and Eng.1883 O.52 r.14.
3 Pruett v. Kohout (M 1995) 171 AR 118, 30 Alta LR(3d) 31.
4 Baker v. Baker (M) 2014 ABQB 710, 70 CPC(7th) 196.

(a) the instructions to the court clerk are not clear,

can be very dangerous for the person ﬁling. See a full discussion of this in R.13.6 n. J. See also

For unknown reasons the Clerk refused to let the defendant ﬁle his defence though the time
had not expired and no one had then yet sought default judgment. Some Clerks’ ofﬁces are
underfunded, understaffed, and overburdened.²
The Court of King’s Bench does not control them.
A supporting afﬁdavit had been presented for ﬁling in the court below by the respondent, but
a bad backlog in the Clerk’s ofﬁce had led to its not being stamped and ﬁled for almost 2 months.
The chambers judge was allowed either to consider date of presentation as the date of ﬁling, or
to consider the delay a curable, technical defect because of the ﬁling backlog, as the Bankruptcy
Act expressly requires where there is no incurable injustice resulting.³
On a commencement

had just expired, and whether the court can change the deemed ﬁling date, see the Sabir case.⁴

13-103
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.42
Maybe in cases of doubt, the Clerk should err on the side of accepting documents for ﬁling; in
any event, that the Clerk accepted it is of little weight if the document is later objected to.¹
A comedy of errors when counsel tried to ﬁle a transcript of a cross-examination on afﬁdavit,
is instructively recited.²
A vast number of Rules call for various written documents. In many cases nothing requires
that they be ﬁled with the Clerk. In some cases the Rules give a form or wording for the document.
So in many cases it is not clear whether or not the document is to be ﬁled with the Court of King’s
Bench. But these Rules seem to require ﬁling; Rr.2.27, 3.34, 3.7, 3.13, 4.15(1) (a), 4.25, 5.27,
5.37, 6.8, 6.11(1), 6.45, 8.6(2), 8.8(6), 9.31, 9.35(1), 10.14(1), 10.18, 10.26(3), 10.39, 10.44,
10.39, 10.43, 11.32, 11.34(3), 13.31, 13.36. Where the Rules give a form, often it has a space for
“Clerk’s Stamp”, but some of those are not filed.
A judge struck two afﬁdavits as they had been ﬁled in court by two persons who were not
parties to the action (the parents of one party), and contained nothing of value.³
There are many ways that a document sent by fax can appear to have gone to the addressee
but not actually arrive or be printed; a number are described.⁴
The Court of King’s Bench website’s
“News & Announcements” part contains a July 27, 2020 notice about fax filing.
The 2020 amendment to R.13.41 allows electronic transactions or ﬁling with the Clerk
without personal attendance, unless circumstances require personal attendance. And it no longer
speciﬁes which means the Clerk may authorize. And now under R.13.41(4) if an afﬁdavit is ﬁled
electronically, the original need not be physically ﬁled with the Clerk, unless the Clerk so requires,
but for some time the person ﬁling, must keep the original afﬁdavit available for inspection on
request.
Covid-19 from March 2020 ended ﬁling in person at the Clerk’s counter, but delays in getting
the relevant documents before a chambers judge persist, despite use of the Court of King’s
Bench’s “SharePoint” electronic platform. More up-to-date procedures are described in the “News
& Announcements” part of the Court of King’s Bench website. See the notes to Rr.6.2 n. A.2, 6.3
and 13.32.
Applications with reserved dates will see Clerks tell counsel or parties to deliver an extra hard
copy of papers relevant to the pending application, to the King’s Bench orderlies’ desk (or security
desk in smaller courthouses), for the use of the judge who will hear the application. (The directive
so to instruct counsel or parties is neither published, nor on the website. )
Absence of Court Clerk
13.42 In the absence or inability of the court clerk or other ofﬁcer of the Court to
act or to do a thing, the duty may be performed by a person designated for that
purpose by the Chief Justice.⁵

13-104
R.13.43 PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES

Seal
13.43 Each court clerk may use the court seal as occasion requires.

Related Provisions
13.14 (endorsements on documents) ; 13.15 (when document is filed).

Duties of Court Clerk
13.44 In addition to any other duties that a court clerk is required by law to
perform, a court clerk has the following duties and responsibilities:
(a) to establish and maintain a court ﬁle for each action started in the
Court and to keep the court ﬁle up to date;
(b) to receive, ﬁle and have custody of all commencement documents,
pleadings, afﬁdavits and records ﬁled in every action, application or
proceeding;
(c) to have custody of all documents required or ordered to be deposited
for safekeeping or otherwise under an order or an enactment;
(d) to take any action or do anything required or permitted under these
rules, an enactment or an order or judgment;
(e) in accordance with the Court’s directions, to keep a detailed log of
court proceedings, including any record that is capable of being
represented or reproduced visually or by sound or both and ensure
that the recording and log are properly and securely stored;
(f) to keep proper accounts and records of money or property received,
paid out or disposed of;
(g) to perform any other functions required by the Minister or the Chief
Justice.¹

order, pleading, property record, rules
Related Provisions
6.13 (duty to record ex parte applications) ; 6.23 (duties of clerk re commission evidence) ; 8.6

(giving notice of trial dates) ; 13.26 (marking exhibits) ; 13.29 (certified copies).
On backlogs in accepting documents submitted for ﬁling, see Rr.3.7 n. J, and 13.41 n.

1 Quite similar to previous 1968 R.719 (parts). It was virtually identical to 1914 R.521; came via 1944 C. R.

13-105
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.45(1)
Notice to be Given to Court Ofﬁcers
13.45(1) Every party or person
(a) who receives an order or judgment, or
(b) in whose favour an order or judgment is made
that imposes duties on a court ofﬁcer must give to the court ofﬁcer written notice
of the order or judgment and of the duty imposed under the order or judgment
unless the Court otherwise orders.
(2) Filing the order or judgment is not notice to the court ofﬁcer.¹

Related Provisions
6.32 (notice to media of publication bans) ; 9.5 (entry of orders) ; 9.8 (service of orders).

Ofﬁcial Court Reporters
13.46(1) An ofﬁcial court reporter must perform the duties required under these
rules or assigned by the Minister or the Court and must
(a) keep in safe custody the record of any proceedings or questioning
taken by the ofﬁcial court reporter,
(b) honestly and accurately transcribe the record of those proceedings
or that questioning, and
(c) deliver a copy of the transcript as required by these rules.²
(2) On or attached to a transcript, the ofﬁcial court reporter must
(a) state the ofﬁcial court reporter’s name,
(b) specify the date and place where the proceedings or questioning
occurred, and
(c) certify the transcript, or the portion of the transcript transcribed, as
complete and accurate.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;143/11]

Related Provisions
5.26 (transcripts of examinations for discovery) ; 6.20 (transcripts of other examinations).

The court will not give a litigant access to the court’s sound recordings of its proceedings just
so that he or she may check the accuracy of the court reporters’ or court transcribers’ transcript,
on a mere suspicion of inaccuracy.³
Nor let the parties make their own sound recordings in court.⁴

1 Quite similar to previous R.721.1, ﬁrst enacted by Alta. Reg.277/95.

1944 C. R.839. A 2002 amendment reﬂected a new broader deﬁnition of “ofﬁcial court reporter”.
3 Shaw v. Belcourt 2011 ABCA 155, [2011] AR Uned 118 (one JA May 13).

13-106
R.13.47 PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
Saskatchewan has a Rule of Court about when a party, or a non-party, to litigation can get a
transcript of proceedings in court.¹
A lawyer refused to allow his client to be examined for discovery unless with real-time court
reporting. The other lawyer objected: it is slow and expensive, transcripts are not certiﬁed and
contain errors, though later a certiﬁed correct transcript comes. The party who objected did not
have to pay the extra fee involved. And if it consumed more time, the objecting party could later
show that, and ask for costs for that extra time. The witness was ordered to attend without real-
time reporting.²
On video recordings in place of transcripts, see R.13.31.
Proof of Ofﬁcial Court Reporter’s Signature Not Required
13.47 Proof of the signature of an ofﬁcial court reporter or a person transcribing
questioning under these rules is not required unless the Court orders otherwise.³

DIVISION 7
PAYMENT INTO COURT AND PAYMENT OUT OF COURT
When Money May be Paid into Court
13.48 Money may be paid into Court in accordance with
(a) these rules;
(b) an enactment;
(c) a judgment or order.

Related Provisions
2.20 (money held by litigation representative) ; 8.3 (deposit for a jury) ; 13.9 (defence of

tender).
How Money is Paid into Court
13.49(1) Money paid into Court must be paid to the court clerk and accompanied
with Form 50.
(2) The court clerk must
(a) give a receipt for money paid into Court, and

Footnote
1 Phillips v. Vo 2023 SKCA 20 (Feb 2).
2 Hill v. Mask (oral M Sep 15) 2017 CarswellAlta 1770, JCC 1501 05866.

PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.50(1)
(b) deposit the money in an account in a bank, treasury branch or trust
corporation, unless otherwise ordered.¹

Tender on Judicial Sale
13.50(1) Certiﬁed cheques received by the court clerk as a tender on a judicial
sale must be held by the court clerk on behalf of the person making the tender.
(2) If the court clerk expects the tender to be considered by the Court or otherwise
dealt with within one month after receipt of the certiﬁed cheque, the court clerk
may hold the cheque without depositing it.
(3) If the court clerk expects that a tender will not be considered or otherwise
dealt with within one month after receipt of the certiﬁed cheque, or if the person
making the tender so requests within one month after making the tender, the court
clerk must invest the money in securities described in rule 13.54 [Investments
and payment earnings].²

Related Provisions
6.64 (sale of personal property) ; 9.32 (sale of secured property) ; 9.38 (sale of land).

A party wanted the court to shield it from its loss of its deposit for not closing a land purchase,
and the Master refused. The contract was a judicial public sale under Court order.³
Litigant’s Account
13.51(1) Money paid into Court must be credited to a litigant’s account for the
action, application or proceeding for which the payment is made.
(2) The court clerk is in charge of every litigant’s account and must record all
transactions related to the account and the authority for the transaction.⁴

13-108
R.13.52(1) PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES
Payments into Court Under Trustee Act
13.52(1) An application for permission to pay money or securities into Court under
the Trustee Act or a payment into Court under that Act must be accompanied
with an afﬁdavit of one or more trustees setting out
(a) a brief description of the trust, the instrument or enactment creating
it or the circumstances under which it arose,
(b) the name of every person interested in or entitled to the money or
securities, together with their addresses, if known, and
(c) an address for service of documents.
(2) Notice of the application or payment into Court must be given to every person
that the Court orders be notiﬁed.¹

Payments Out of Court
13.53 Money paid into Court may be paid out of Court only
(a) if the court clerk certiﬁes that the money is in Court,
(b) in accordance with an order, unless otherwise permitted by the
Court,
(c) by cheque signed and countersigned by a person designated by the
Minister, and
(d) if the payment is to
(i) the person entitled to the money or the person’s lawyer, or
(ii) another person speciﬁed by the Court or named by the
person entitled to the money.²

Related Provisions
6.27 (giving notice before distributing funds in court) ; 6.56 (interpleader orders).

The Canada Revenue Agency served a Requirement to Pay (like a garnishee summons) on
the Clerk of the Court in Alberta for funds he was holding. The Court of King’s Bench later did
order that certain of the funds be paid to the Canada Revenue Agency, but the Canada Revenue
Agency’s procedure was not proper. Indeed it could be contempt of court, as it threatened the
Clerk and the Court with personal liability if funds were paid to the debtor company. The court had

13-109
PART 13: TECHNICAL RULES R.13.54(1)
earlier instructed the Clerk (before service) to pay out only on court order. Therefore the clerk was
not “liable to make payment” to the debtor company within the meaning of the Income Tax Act and
could not be garnished.¹
Investments and Payment Earnings
13.54(1) Money paid into Court or money subject to an order may be invested on
the court clerk’s initiative or on the written request of a person having an interest
in the money, and if the money is invested, the money may only be invested in
(a) public funds of Alberta or Canada,
(b) deposit certiﬁcates of a bank, treasury branch or trust corporation,
or
(c) securities or a class of securities authorized by the Court.
(2) Money earned on investments made under subrule (1) must be paid to the
person entitled to the money paid into Court when the principal amount is paid
out, unless the Court otherwise orders.²

Disposition of Money in Accounts
13.55(1) If the balance remaining to the credit of a court account is less than
$100 and 2 years has passed after the amount was deposited in the account
without the balance being claimed, the account must be closed by transferring
the balance to the suspense account maintained by the court clerk.
(2) Subject to subrule (1), 10 years after the last payment into a court account,
the balance must be transferred to the suspense account.
(3) Money in the suspense account, including interest or any return on money in
that account, is subject to the control of, and may be paid out only in accordance
with a direction of, the Lieutenant Governor in Council.
(4) This rule does not affect the right of a person entitled to the money in the
suspense account, including the right to recover the money.³
